I'VIeDOUGALLS Pategt sELF-RAISIN6 PLOUR MagoN The Examiner Saturday, October 20,1904.

TEIE "BOLTON- SCHOOLS.

mittee, and when they got them through, or rather during the time they were getting them through. thee would have to have the inspector down to hold thy inquiry. In the meantime they would be pushing for- ward with another batch. He would like the Council to know (holding up a great sheaf of documents) what a largeamount of work all these-arrangements entailed, and he might say—as he said on every opportunity— that most of this work would have been done by now had it not been for that foolish resolution of the Council in extending the time for payment to seven years. He did not hesitate to say that that resolution had considerably handicapped them is their work. Alderman HUTCHINSON secondektho adoption of the minutes. Councillor PEMBERTON said be would like to dissoci- ate himself from Councillor Tiranion's remark about the Council's" foolish "resolution. He did not agree that it was a foolish resolution. Councillor Tinnion's opinion was not the opinion of the committee. • Councillor TINNION: It was my personar opinion. . Councillor Pcumwros., continuing, did not agree that it was so much a cause of delay as Councillor Tinnion suggested, and in his opinion it ought to be ten years instead of seven. Councillor ELLISON asked that the Paving and Sewerage Committee would include in its list of streets for treatment the passage behind Hewitt-street, Latch- ford. It was and had been for some time in a sad ' condition. Councillor TismoN said they would put it in the next batch, but he did not think it possible to get it in the present batch. He differed from Councillor Pemberton respecting the seven years resolution, and thought it did cause considerable delay. The minutes were approved. CROMWELL STATUE. At a meeting of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee, on the 29th September, it was resolved that in consequence of the public persisting in making walks across the recreation ground, the Kendrick-street (West) gate be closed until further noticl. It was further decided that the statue of Oliver Cromwell be repainted and that estimates be obtained for the re- painting of the Walker fountain. The MAYOR, in moying the confirmation of the minutes alluded to the decision to repaint the Crom- well statue. Alderman SMETHURST seconded. Alderman Bennett rose to speak. The MAYOR: You are not going to oppose the re- painting I hope? (Laughter.) Alderman BENNETT said he was, on the other hand, very pleased. He pointed out, however, that the Crom- , well statue was very much disfigured and spoiled by ' the electric lamp in front, and •he wished they could move it. Councillor TINNION : The statue? Alderman BErsErr : No, the lamp. The MAYOR: I thought that statue was needed there to keep the Academy in order. (Laughter.) The minutes were then confirmed. LABOURERS WAGES. Councillor &row referred to a letter contained in the minutes, of the Tramway's Committee and written by himself in which he stated he had been informed that in the repairing of the War- rington tramcars, labourers who received 6d. per hour were doing fitter's work. The question had been raised by the West Ham secretary of the Amalgamated Society of Engineers who had received no acknowledgment of a letter he had written asking for information. Councillor MAGINN objected to the interference of a third party between the Corporation and their servants. It appeared to him that they as a council were not going to be allowed to manage their local affairs,but that the town was going to be regulated by a society of trade unionists in West Ham. He did not think they were en- titled to give information to peoplein West Ham or any .where else. (Hear, hear). Alderman SMETHURST said the Tramways Committee were constantly pestered by pettifogging people send- ing letters of that sort. They had had people writing to know who was painting the trams, and who was putting the varnish on the paint, all because of such men as they had in the Council. Councillor STorr : Yon have no right to make such insinuations. The IVIA.You : He has not named you personally. Continuing Alderman SMETHURST said he thought it was quite time the Council' took it upon themselves to conduct their business in a proper business manner and not to let first one and then another come interfering between their managers and the men they employed. (Hear, hear.) The minutes were approve d. WARRINGTON BRIDGE WIDENING. A SCENE.

I At a meeting of the Sites and Buildings Committee I it was resolved to invite architects, practising in War- rington, to furnish competitive designs for the new, "Bolton " schoels to be erected in Latchford. It was I resolved that the site for the proposed new "Bolton" 1 schools be putchased outright. Councillor Tr*sro& proposed and Alderman Smethurst- teConded th 6 itclOtition of the minutes. The Rev. J. YONGE said he would like to ask a qiiastion ,*hich a great many people in town were ! askihg, and which a distinguished visitor to the town I had 'asked in a very emphatic way, in fact, in so I enWitatie a Manner that he thought it desirable that it should be mentioned at that meeting. The question Ivis as'tla *hat was being done towards the provision of ttliel(e'v'tsChobls in the Borough which had been enter- fitilied'st/lbitg. He asked the question with a sense of -doirthidP-ble responsibility because he felt that unless te Sifts and Bnildings Committee woke up on the 'question they might receive a waking up which might not be very pleasant, because he believed that one of the inspectors was disposed to ask some very pertinent questions upon the matter. There seemed to be in some minds the imprassion that there had been an unnecessary delay in making these provisions and if that had been the case and there had been no ground for it, it might lead to consequences, especially if pressure was brought to bear by the Board of Education, which would not be , particularly enjoyed. Councillor TINNION said that if the committee , possessed Aladdin's lamp the schools would be provided at once, but as it was the committee could not move any quicker than they were doing. The matter had to be approached very carefully, especially when they con- sidered that they were undertaking work of which they had no experience in Warrington. As not a single indi- vidual of the committee had had any preliminary experi- ence they were endeavouring to learn something about the erection of schools, and with that object in view they had inspected sohools in Manchester, Salford, Birkenhead, and Liverpool, and had thereby learnt the sVle of school which was most satisfactory, whilst being least costly. They had asked for competitive designs from local architects, and they hoped that-by the 16th January to have such information and plans as would be suitable to • the whole of the borough and to be in the position to proceed with the erection of the schools as soon as the weather would allow. There had so far been no unnecessary delay and the committee were determined not to be rushed in the matter. They had tried to give as much satisfaction as they could and he ! thought that when the ratepayers learnt the amount of I work they had done, they would give the Sites and Buildings Committee credit for carrying the matter for- ward as fast as was possible under the circumstances. Mr. MONKS pointed out that some little delay had , been caused by the difficulty of the getting of sites. , Now, however negotiations were alsocompleted for the purchase of three or four more sites for schools. , Alderman SMETHURST said he could endorse what was said by Councillor Tinnion. The sub-committee had done all they possibly could. He :could inform the , committee that they had purchased:a site upon which

erecting new schools not having been begun, but it was well-known five years before that with regard to school accommodation the town had suffered most severely, and For that reason he associated himself with the remarks made by Mr. May. • Mr. PLINSTON said that the School Attendance Com- mittee were constantly receiving complaints of. the overcrowding of schools. The School Attendance Com- mittee were doing their best, and he hoped that before long the Sites and Buildings Committee would bring in a scheme for the erection of new schools, and go on with the work.

Mr. J. D. BUCKTON explained Mr. Upson's statement by pointing out that some schools which were formerly well attended were now partially empty as the children had gone into other neighbourhoods and helped to fill the schools in those districts to a point of overcrowd- ing. With all due respect to the gentlemen on the Sites and Buildings Committee he did not think that committee had worked as promptly as they might have done. If a voluntary school h‘d been contemplated it would have been erected long fag° and, moreover the money would have been raised for its erection. The Board of Education had long ago asked for the erection of a school at Orford and the matter had not yet, to all appearances, been taken in hand. Councillor TINNION said he thought some of the gentlemen who had spoken had only done so because the local papers would be rather blank on account•of the shortness of the meeting. Mr. MONKS: I cannot admit that. _ _

Councillor rINSION said he could not help making the remark as it was distinctly suggested to his mind. He did not admit there had been any unnecessary delay. He knew there were a good many people who could do a thing a great deal better than those who were appointed to do it. He had learnt that from past experience in Warrington and other places. There may have been some delay in providing sites but upon that matter they had to be very careful as a good many people were ever ready to buy sites cheap and then sell dear to the Corporation or Education Authority. There were many sites which the committee had thought equally desirable. They would like to put up five schools, but asit was a question of money they would have to be content with two. rho Bolton school at Latchford would be the first to be proceeded with, while the school at Orford would be taken in hand immediately. It was in the obtaining of sites that the delay had been caused and in that direction the cora- mittee could not have moved much quicker as they did not wish to pay through the nose for any site they wished to purchase. (Hear, hear.) The discussion then ended and the minutes of the committee were approved.

TOWN COUNCIL

SUGGESTED OPEN-AIR SWIMMING

BATHS

Lively Discussions.

The Mayor (Alderman Bolton) presided at the meeting of the Town Council for the municipal year Nshich was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening. There were also present : —Aldermen A. Bennett, John Evans, R. W. Francoinb, T. Hutchinson, W. Pierpoint, J. Smethurst and T. H. Sutton ; Councillors T. H. Ashcroft, T. Barton W. Brough, T. Burton, J. G. Craik, S. Davies D. S. Duncan, James Evans, L. Ellison, W. J. Forshaw, T. J. Hulse, J. Lord, J. Maginn, A. Peacock, P. Peacock, J. Pemberton, J. Sankey, F. Stott, H. Thornton, D. Tinnion, J. Wilkinson, W. J. Wilson, and T. - , COUNCILLOR DODDS' RESIGNATION.

The' MAYOR reported that he had received the resignation of Councillor James Dodds as one of the representatives of the Town Hall Ward and declared the office to be vdcant. VACANCY ON THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE. The Council appointed Councillor Sankey a repre- sentative member of the Education Committee in Mr. Dodds's place. EFFICIENT POLICE FORCE.

At a meeting of the Watch Committee on October 13th, the following certificate of efficiency of the police from Mr. A. Akers-Douglas, one of H.M.'s principal Secretaries of State, was produced :—" I hereby certify that the police force of the Borough of Warrington has been maintained in a state of efficiency in point of num- bers and discipline for the year ending the 29th of September, 1904." The minutes ware passed, on the motion of the MAYOR, seconded by Alderman FRANCOMB. LOANS REQUIRED. PRIVATE STREET WORKS.

===== 0002601_19041029_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
The Finance and Estate Committee has received the sanction of the Local Government Boardto the borrow- ing of various sums for private street works except so far as the application relates to the passage behind Nos. 16 to 38, Manchester-road. The Board announce that they defer their decision upon the application so far as it relates to the sum of £l2O 16s. 4d. in respect of the passage referred to until they have been informed that the objection to the proposed works had been settled. A resolution has been passed by the committee to the effect that the Town Clerk and Borough Treasure t be authorized to wait upon the Local Government Board with respect to certain loans. —At a meeting on October 17th, it was resolved that the Town Council be instructed to apply to the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing of the following sums, being the amount in each case of excess expenditure :—Baths extension, £380; Police Buildings. £4,506 ;Technical Institute, £6,130; infectious Diseases Hospital, Aikin-street, £1,016; Bridge-Street improvement, £7.483. Alderman BENNETT moved the confirmation of the minutes. Councillor A. PEACOCK seconded, and the moon was carried. ELECTRICITY V. INCANDESCENT LIGHTS. The Street Improvement and Lighting Committee re- commended "That two four-light incandescent gas lamps be fixed in Bridge-street in place of the electric lamp recently removed. The Ordnance Survey Department having intimated that they were about to make a re- survey of the borough, and offering to revise the large scale surveys at cost to the town of 245. per acre, after discussion thereon the offer was delined.—lt was resolved "That this committee approve of the appointment of Mr. Thomas Crompton for surveying and completing the ordnance map of the borough, and that this com- mittee will be prepared to pay one-half his wages."—At another meeting Messrs. Peter Walker and Son,Limited having offered to give to the Corporation a piece of land at the come; of Pinner's-brow and Silver-street, containing about 107 5•9 square yards, required for the • completion of the widening of Silver-street. on condi- tion that a piece of land of equal area and value be given to the company as part of the proposed site of the Lord Rodney public-house, it was resolved that the offer be accepted and the conditions agreed to. Alderman BENNETT moved the confirmation of the minutes. Councillor Ev,vss. seconded. Alderman SMETHURST opposed the recommendation with reference to incandescent lights, and read a letter from Mr. Matthias,the borough electrical and tramways engineer,lcontending that;the committee's action would be a retrograde one. It was universally acknowledged that efficient street lighting could only be carried out by arc lamps, and he also objected to the introduction of incandescent lights on the score of economy. Councillor EVANS said there had been considerable complaint about the lighting of Bridge-street. From time to time requests had been made for a cheapening of the arc lights in the streets, but the committee were slow to accede to what were considered to be reason- able terms of agreement. The arc lamps were turned down about midnight, and they got simply two little lights to denote that there was a stump there and that no one should run against it. (Laughter.) That was about all the light they got. It was considered that the incandescent light was cheaper and its efficiency was being seen all over the town. They wore beinc, tried in different pla,ces. Why should they pay for lighting by electricity as a sort of novelty when the incandescent light was cheaper? Why should they pay more at the ratio of £2O to £l2 when they could do efficiently with gas. (Hear, hear.) After some further discussion the minute was with- drawn. The other minutes were agreed to. SANKEY BOOK WATER. SEWAGE FILTRATION.

At a meeting of the Sanitary Works Committee, on October 11, the report of the Public Analyst on the treatment of Sankey Brook water by means of the Dunbar system of purification now being worked on a small scale at the Longford Depot was read. It stated that the committee would no doubt be interested to know how the Dunbar bed was treating the Sankey Brook water used for condensing purposes. The water was first turned on to the bed on Monday, September 26th, at the rate of about 30,000 galls, in the 24 hours, and it ran continuously night and day till Friday night, September 30th. Running at the extremely high rate of 50,000 galls daily the results were not quite so good ; still the effluents showed a purification of over 70 per cent,and they were in all cases clear and bright and free from smell. The following letter from Messrs. Martin and Company, 44, Parliament-street, Nottingham, and addressed to the Town Clerk, was read :—" As the trial pleat which we erected in Warrington for the purpose of demonstrating our system of sewage filtration has been running for something like seven months, and we understand giving satisfaction, we should esteem it a favour on your part if you would kindly inform us as early as possible whether you intend availing yourself of the privilege orerecting the system under the terms of our agreement." It was resolved that the 'lowa Clerk be instructed to reply to the letter, stating that the committee were of opinion that a six months trial of the existing bed should be made before definitely deciding as to the extension of the system. These minutes were approved, on the motion of Alder- man EvAtis, seconded by Alderman SMItTHURST, the former remarking that so far the Dunbar system was going on very satisfactorily. STREET PAVING.

-- - The Paving and Sewerage Committee reported that the Borough Surveyor had submitted specifications, plans, and sections' estimates and provisional appor- tionments of the cost of forming and paving a number of streets. It was resolved" That tie same be approved, and that the Town Clerk serve notices requiring each of tbe streets to be formed and paved in pursuance of the Private Street Works Act,1892.11 was also resolved that application be made to the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing of the sum of £5,2.;1 128.10 d. for private street works. The Borough Surveyor reported that he had seen a representative of the London and North-Western Railway Company with reference to the paving of the passage leading from Froghall-lane to Bewsey-road, who stated that the com- pany could not see their way tolagree to the suggestions of the committee, and it was resolved "that the Borough Surveyor be instructed to serve a notice on the com- pany calling ,upon them to forthwith put the passage in good repair.' Councillor £INNION proposed the adoption of the minutes. He said the committee had been in com- munication with the London and North-Western Rail- way with reference to the permanent paving of the passage from Froghall-lane to Bewsey-road, and made such overtures to them which they thought would be acceptable. The company had not at present agreed to their suggestions, so they bad ordered the Borough Surveyor to serve them with a notice to put the passage in order. Whether the company would merely cinder the passage or not he could not say, but the passage belonged to them and he did not think the Council had any power to compel them to put down a permanent passage. They had, however, not given up hope yet of arranging something with the company. They had had submitted specifications, plans and sections for about 44 streets or portions of streets and passages, and had instructed" the Town Clerk to serve the necessary notices, and were also making application to the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing of £5,251 12s. 10d. This, asbe intimated, was a further instalment of those streets and passages that were having the carefal and constant attention of the corn- .-

The General Purposes Committee at a meeting on October 17th reported the rejection of Messrs. Fair- dough's offer and resolved to apply to the Local Government Board for sanction to borrow £3,263, to proceed with the widening of the bridge. The MAYOR moved and Alderman FRA.NOOMB seconded the confirmation of thel minutes, which Was agreed to. Alderman SUTTON rose to speak but was unable to get a hearing, being greeted with cries of "Chair " when he attempted to address the meeting. Councillor Maginn also rose but met with the same fate as Alderman Sutton his utterances- being rendered inaudible by the repeated cries of "Chair." the Council being for some minutes in a state of uproar.

OPEN AIR BATHS. LIVELY DISCUSSION. ANOTHER SCENE: Councillor STOTI' had given notice of his intention to move :—"That it be a recommendation from this Council to the Town Hall, Parks, and Baths Committee, and the Open Spaces Sub-committee to take into con- sideration the provision of an open-air swimming bath for the Orford Ward Recreation Ground." The MA.YOR said the committee were willing to take the matter into consideration. Councillor STorr : If you make that promise it is sufficient for me. Councillor A. PEACOCK remarked that he thought Orford Ward was not suited to the provision of an open-air bath. Councillor BURTON asked that the committee should at the same time consider the advisability of construct- ing an open-air bath in the neighbourhood of Liver- pool-road. The M.tYoR intimated that every renreation ground in the town would be taken into consideration. Councillor STorr then formally moved his motion. Councillor PEMBERTON seconded and the motion was acreed to. Councillor Trxmox rose to speak but wis greeted with cries of "Chair" and was ruled out of order by the Mayor. Councillor Tinnion persisted that he had a right to speak. He was, he said, on his feet before the motion was carried. ...Alderman SUTTON: So was I. Chair, chair. The MAYOR : Gentlemen, you are getting a little out of band. Councillor TINNION : It is you Mr. Mayor who is getting a little previous. Alderman SurToN : You would not stick up for me. Councillor TINNION : I would have done so wily— (Cries of "Chair.") The Council then, on being asked by the Mayor, con- sented to hear Councillor Tinnion. Councillor TINNION : I submit I have a perfect right to speak without the consent of the Council. I was standing before the motion was accepted. Continuing he pointed out that there were other sites for an open air bath which were more suitable than Orford Ward. There was a field behind the cricket ground at Arpley where there was already what he might describe as a , natural bath. The MAYOR : With a dead dog in it. Councillor TINNION : Really, Mr. Mayor, I must call von to order. Continuing, he said he had seen as many as 50 children in that open-air bath. It was free from danger, and it showed that the children had greater 1 sense than many members of the Council, because they had already provided themselves with an open-air bath. The existing bath. could be converted into an open-air bath with little trouble and at little expense, and he for one would welcome the proposal to convert it. Councillor BARTON thought Mill-lane should also be taken into consideration, as in the summer they always had a number of drowning fatalities in that neighbour- hood. The MAYOR intimated that Mill-lane would also be considered, and the matter dropped. THE MAYOR THANKED. Alderman SUTTON moved :—"That the best thanks of this Council be presented to William Bolton, Esq., •J P . for the satisfactory manner in which he has discharged the responsible duties devolving upon him as mayor and chief magistrate of the borough during. his year of office, and for the unvarying courtesy and impartiality displayed by him when presiding over the deliberations of the Council,and in his intercourse with its members." In doing so he said he thought the fact that the Mayor had attended more meetings during the year than any other member of the Council was in itself sufficient proof that he had taken a deep interest in the work of every committee by making himself personally acquainted with the work of the committees. As a result he had been enabled to give very valuable assist- ance upon all matters considered by the Council. (Hear, hear.) In his capacity as Mayor he had acted imparti- ally and had made them all toe the line. (Applause.) He coupled with the vote the name of the Mayoress. (Applause). Councillor TINNION seconded and remarked that in the matter of attendances the Mayor had established a record. He had had a very difficult time to face in the year of his mayoralty but he thought they would agree that Alderman Boltonhad conducted the business of the Council in 'a manner which had reflected credit upon himself and upon the Council as a whole. (Hear hear.) He hoped they would all remember the very difficult position which a Mayor occupied, and show their thanks I to Alderman Bolton by individually and collectively ' doing everything which lay in their power to assist him in the carrying out of his Glides. (Applause.) The vote was heartily and unanimously accorded. ' The MAYOR brieflTreturned thanks, and thanked the members of the Council and the officials of the Corpor- ation for the kindness and consideration which had been , extended to him on all occasions. (Hear, hear.)

PENKETH.

DARING BURGLARY AT SANKEY.—The premises of Messrs. W. H. Ireland and Co. Cycle Manufacturers, were forcibly entered on Monday night. The premises were safely secured when left on Monday, but on Tuesday morning a workman found that a burglary had been perpetrated. Mr. Ireland was summoned, and it was found that two new cycles had been removed front the front window, and also a quantity of accessories. The office had been ransacked, and the thieves had evidently been very methodical in their proceedings. Several tools, chisels, gltc., had been left behind, but the police have not yet discovered the persons con- cerned. The value of the goods taken is about £3O, and the news created a mild sensation on Tuesday in the neighbourhood. MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT ASSOCIATION.—The opening of the session on Wednesday was celebrated by the usual invitation to the members by the President, this office being held this session by Mr. W. Birch. A good number of the members were present, and the evening was pleasantly spent in yorel and instrumental music, and discussing the coming session. Refreshments were handed round, and the session promises to be a success- ful oae. ST MARY'S MISSION.-011 Tuesday .night, a lecture illustrated with lantern views, was gyve* in the above room. The subject was one of interest at the present juncture, being "Japan and the Japanese." There was a large attendance, and the lecture was listened to with great attention. A collection was taken to defray expenses.

A dream—a painter's dream! He had seen I scores of beautiful women in this classic land, hut I ; she surpassed them all. The Monastery of Monte ' Cassino had brought him to this spot, but far more worth the visit was Lucia. ,He would have come from the other side of the world jii, to gaze 1 at her. Such beauty never had lived and iiieathed before. By day and by night her face and her form . were with him. He would reproduce this beauty : on the canvas, and it would bring him fame. Lucia was an orphan. Her mother had died at . her birth; her father, the following_ year. Her : grandmother had brought her up. Marianna was old, selfish, and ill-tempered, and cared little or nothing for her daughter's child. Lucia was not one of the happiest. However, there would soon be a change, she told herself. Her betrothed would return in the winter, and then she would ' marry and have a house of her own. She had been a promised wife for four years—ever since Vincenzo hsd gone to the army. He was a noble-looking fellow, this Vincenzo of hers, handsome of face , and tall of stature, and, although only peasant- born, by his haughty bearing appeared conscious of , being a descendant of the old Masters of the world. : Lucia was proud of being his love, for all the girls • of her acquaintance envied her. A well-matched pair they would make, their elders said, the finest young man and the most beautiful maiden in the pro- vince of Caserta. Lucia bad loved Vincenzo all her life. To be sure she did not love so fiercely and jealously as he did; still she loved, and 'perhaps she would be happier, she thought, than if she put him before everything in the world. Such love as that only brought misery; she had seen it. Her friend Rosa hail thought, before she married him, that there was no one else in all the wide world half so good or so handsome as Antonio. She had put him before everybody and everything. Now she was changed—they both were changed—Rosa and Antonio. They were not so much in love with each other. Antonio's brows were often knit in ' anger, and Rosa's tongue had grown sharp. They had been too passionately in love to last so. Lucia almost wished that Vincenzo loved her less, and then perhaps, he would not change. When Bertram Finnes begged old Marianna to permit him to make a picture of her grand-daughter, she demurred. - "I do not ask the favour without intending to pay for it," he said, taking out his well-filled purse and handing it to her. The expression of her face, and the tone of her voice, instantly changed. "This moment, Signore, as soon as it shall please you, she will wait upon you. Lucia, you' idle one, come forward. Ah, I shall soon get rid of her, Madonna be praised! " continued the old hag. "I won't weep when that Vincenzo of hers takes her away. But here she is, holding her head as high as any Contessa in the city of Rome beyond. She is proud of her face. It won't last I long, it won't last long, the beauty that turns the heads of all the men, young and old. Let her be a wife and a mother ''for a few years. Where will it be? I was a beauty once. Look at me now. The children point at me and call me the devil's wife, then run away, for my face—the face of the once lovely . Marianna —frightens them. If I put, out my hands to take a babe from the arms of its mother, it shrieks in terror, and buries its face in its mother's neck. And yet I was as beautiful as she. But she will change as I have changed, for nothing lasts, no, nothing lasts—nor youth, nor beauty, nor love —nothing except hate; that lasts as long as we last. I hate everyone who has what I have not, yet what I desire. I hate. Santa Maria I how I can hate, and she can do that too. That is all that will be , left to her. Youth, and beauty, and love—all will leave her, but hatred will remain, and grow in strength with increase of years. But here she is, make a picture of her while you may." and the hideous old woman hobbled away to count out the contents of the purse that Bertram Finnes had given her. "An unpleasant old creature," lie thought, as he arranged his easel. "Not the sweetest-tempered old lady in the world, I should think. Signorina," he said to Lucia. . "The .old are seldom sweet-tempered," she replied. "It seems to me that life has dis- appointed them and soured their dispositions. You will find many old women here at Cassino like my grandmother.' For days and weeks Bertram Finnes worked on the canvas that was to bring him fame; at the same time he worked into the heart of the simple peasant girl. It was, not often that she thought of 'Vincenzo now; when she did the thought was that she loved this tawny-haired Englishman so much more than him. This Signor Bertram, this painter who told her that she was a goddess, a young Juno, he was so different from any man she had ever seen before. She did not know exactly what be meant by a goddess and a Juno. It was some- thing in praise of her beauty, of course, for he never wearied of telling her that she, was a feast for his eyes. And now she dreamt of life until the last hour passed with him. What a Heaven on earth it would be. Now she could well understand that passionate . love which put • the beloved before 'everybody and everything else in thi. world. Rosa had loved Antonio like this, and it had come to her. to love like this—but not Vincenzo. - There WA EA no likeness in the love she bore her betrothed to that which she bore this Englishman.. Had she had a brother, she would have. loved him as she loved Vincenzo, but what she felt for Signor Bertram she had never experienced, nor thought of, nor dreamt of, until now. Ah, Rosa's love had changed, it had cooled, was almost extinguished. Her grandmother said it was ever so—marriage killed love. With the man, I possession' extinguished the fire of love; With thefl I woman, the dead ashes remained. But no, it could ' not be ever so; it would not change for her and for him. He had told her his was unalterable— undying. One day he said to her: "Lucia, my beautiful one, I will not work more upon this picture here at Cassino, but take it to London before the winter and finish it there. I will exhibit it in the spring." She grew white and statue,que. "Why that look, Lucia?" "Must you go away ? " she asked, tremulously. 'Not without you, bell' id,010," he replied. Ho often used that expression of endearment, which sounded to her ears so much sweeter from his lips than from those of Vincenzo. ! "You will come with me ?" He strained her to his breast and kissed her repeatedly. "Vincenzo! I am the promised wife of Vincenzo," she said, trying to disengage herself , from: his arms.

_ He restrained her. "What, would you let him stand between you and me—let him destroy our future t " _

"I may not ; no, _no :1 will not be false to him," she exclaimed, wrenoiing herself from his etnbrace. "I have been his proniised wife for four years, and he believes me to be true." "Would you be true to give your hand to him, while I possessed your heart, your love. No, no, Lucia ; we belong to melt other by right of our love." She walked away front him without another word. At Ave Maria he sought her again and repeated his perruasions. 'Would you, my idol, my.goddess, my beantiful queen, prefer to bury yourself here with that taasant fellow than to come with me into the great world and be adored by all' who could appreciate perfect beauty? Lucia, you say yourself that you never , loved him as you love me, and I verily believe the little love you once had for hint has lessened considerably of late.. 'Think no more of him, but come with me away from Cassino, Caserta, Italy, before he leaves the army." She shook her regal heid. "I may not, I may not." The following morning, without another word to Lucia, Bertram Finiies left for Rome, and did not return to Casino for six .or eight weeks. ll', would try what a short absence would do: Re would not give her up lightly. It would be sink a triumph to take this superb creature back with him to London—to take back with him the original of the picture that was to raise him to the pinnacle of fame. In the meantime a change came over Lucia. She thought. Bertram had left her for ever. It seemed to her that the world had suddenly grown dark and cold. All the radiance and sparkle died out of her face, • her head was bent, her step languished; her voice sad. "Bestir yourself, girl," raid old Marianna, "don't go about dreaming of that painting man who made love to you for his amusement, and haa now gone off to do the same thing to soute other foolish one like yourself. It is well for you and for him that Vincenzo did not come back while he was here and see his lips pressed to yours, and hear the honeyed words he poured into your ear. And it was a willing ear too. Cold steel would have settled it." Lucia went but stoke not..

- . She rose frthn the low seat on which she bad been lounging, stretched forth her hands entreat- ingly, reeled, then fell back in a dead swoon. "I had to do it," muttered Bertram Finnes look- ing down at the beautiful creature he had so injured. "I had to do it, and now it is all over."

'Show Sunday had come and gone. All who had seen Bertram Finnes's picture said it was a great work;* it would take the public by surprise • it would bring him a fame, immediate and far- reaching. "How beautiful your picture is, Bertram," said his promised wife, a little fair, blue-eyed, fluffy- haired girl. "But tell me ; who is she ?" • "Nobody, my dear Mabel." "Nobody 1 Is it only an imaginary face; purely your own conception ? " "Well, not exactly, it bears•a slight resemblance to an Italian model—a Roman woman—known at the schools."

"Some people are whispering strange things about the original of your picture," tho girl said, shyly. "That she is-"

"Spare me, sweet one. Our originals are always somebodies. I know the style of the little romances that take the round. I send it in to-morrow. Do you know I.have grown fond of my creation, and I shall miss it from my studio. Yes, ' The Dreamer' goes to Burlington House to-morrow." "I am so proud, intensely proud of you, Bertram. All the world will ring with your praise. They will say that you are the greatest of living English painters." .

- He stooped and kissed the little up-turned face. When Bertram returned to his home that night, he was informed by his housekeeper that a visitor waited him in the studio.

"A visitor at this time of night," he exclaimed, in astonishment, "and in the studio? You have done very wrong, Mrs. Martin, to allow anyone to wait there." "It is the Signora, sir. I thought,you wouldn't mind her waiting. for you in the studio. She said she wished to, wait there." "Not the Signora Lucia. She returned to Italy a month ago, as you know." "Yes, sir, the Signora Lucia,. dressed like a Roman woman of the people, but so thin and worn. You will hardly recognise her." A fearful scowl overspread his face as he strode into the studio. He looked around. There Wa6 no one visible.

"Where are you, Lucia?" he cried. There was no reply. He was angry, and roughly drew aside curtains, portieres, screens, easels where she might be hidden. . "There is no one here, Mrs. Martin," he called to his old housekeeper. "How strange t" she exclaimed-. "The Signori must have let herself out without my hearing her." Bertram Finnes drew a' long breath of relief. He was a moral. coward, and dreaded to %meet his victim face to face again. "And so they have begun to talk already," he thought. "Some of them have seen the original of The Dreamer,' that is sure. But what do I cares so long as Mabel does not believe? My own con-. ception, she asked. No! No mortal man could conceive a beauty such as that. ' He went over to the eauel on 'which was his picture, "The Dreamer," and drew aside the velvet curtain that hung before it. _

Had he stichh'nly gone mad, or were his eyes playing him a trick? Where was the picture that was to carry his name through unborn ages? Ha gazed upon a canvas ripped and torn into shreds -- all that remained of his labour, his ambition, hi. hopes, his dreams. 0 Heavens! What a revenge!" he groaned, as with hit; hands he shut out from his eyes the sigb! of the destruction. "To have taken my life walla' have bean less cruel."

• She smiled at him, not having an idea of what wag coming. .

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King is understood to be very pleased with the picture of the Coronation upon which Mr. E. A. Abbey, R.A., has been occupied for the past two years. It represents the actual crowning ceremony in the Abbey and measures about 16 feet by 10 feet. This vast canvas was only recently finished and submitted to his Majesty, who is delighted with the work. It will be on view to the public during November at the Honover Galleries, New Bond-street, London, when no doubt many people will take advantage of the opportunity to see this historic picture which has been painted by one of the ablest artists of the present day. It will afterwards be placed in Buckingham Palace or Windsor Castle. The Queen and Sunday Observance.—The Queen's return from Denmark was delayed firstly owing to the illness of the Empress Dowager. of Russia, and secondly onaccount of bad weather, but her Majesty likes the old fashioned Sunday "at home," and always avoids travel- ling on the Sabbath if possible. To her it is a day of rest, and no doubt the Queen shares the opinion that Societyhas gone too far in turning it into a day of entertainment and holiday-making. The popular idea now seems to be to spend the Sunday as far from home as possible, and those who protest against Sunday entertainments and amusements, are voted hopelessly dull and dowdy. Few probably would wish to go back to the dreary dulness of the early Victorian Sabbath and even in Scotland there has been considerable reaction from that state of things, but many people think we have gone to the other extreme and that we are not very far off the Sunday as it is spent in France and other continental countries. Tattlers All.—Afternoon tea has hitherto been regarded as one of the comparatively harmless amuse- ments of Society, but it seems to be viewed by a cer- tain curate as no better than playing Bridge, going to the races, the theatres, and other dissipations. He com- plains that he has been driven from his appointment by, "the gossip at afternoon teas." This he described as a moral and infectious disease, and its chief place was that" pernicious institution of afternoon tea, where the idle women do chiefly collect for nothing else but gossip." It was, he said, the "special playground of the devil," and it is to be concluded from this diatrible that the rev, gentleman must have been the subject of some adverse criticism amongst his parishioners. The London Revival.—London has settled down to its winter season and on the fine days the shops are crowded with customers on the look-out for anything that is new and attracive in matters of dress. People are back from Scotland and .foreign pleasure resorts, and there appears to be less evidence of that general want of money of which shopkeepers have complained for so long. The theatres and restaurants are crowded, whilst Society weddings are numerous, and there is a general revival of entertaining. Amongst afternoon amusements Bridge still retains first place, but ping- pong and all parlour ball games, have been absolutely an bished from the drawing-room. Less Ostentatious Display.—lt is surprising how few toilettes the Society trousseau contains nowadays. Prospective brides seem to have discovered that it is a langp mistake to stock themselves with gowns it would be impossible to wear out, before they became hope- lessly old-fashioned. And yet another welcome note of economy is apparently Spreading, forlashionable dress- makers are complaining that their customers no longer deem it impossible to appear more than two or three times in the same toilette. That Society women are wearing their clothes longer than they have done, for very many years past—indeed since the craze for osten- tataously advertising the extensiveness of their ward- robes, became the correct thing—is a fact, and in this ,1 they are but following the example of our Queen, and I the Princess of Wales, also of many of our great dames, who appear again and again in the same gowns. The New Jerseys.—The knitted and crochetted golf jerseys are this season more fascinating than ever I before. Some open over a simulated waistcoat and show lapels faced with some contrasting colour, others button to the throat where they have turned down collars of some bright hue, but whatever style, they are one and all dressy and smart. The sleeves follow the lines of the new tailor ones. They are plain, and almost tightly fitting, though not aggressively so. For wear With a tailor-made costume, with or without the coat, these new jerseys are particularly suitable and their light clinging warmth can hardly be over-estimated. In price they are more moderate than was the case a year ago and with care they could he made to last for two or three seasons. About Fars.—A few seasons back we had compara- tively little choice in the matter of furs. There were sable, ermine, chinchilla, sealskin, bear, fox, skunk, and a very few, other varieties, and then the:selection ended. Now we have a perfectly bewilderin4 assortment of furs laid before us, and the very misleading names bestowed upon many of the new specimens, makes the conaision worse than ever. Monkey skin for example is presented under various high sounding titles, rabbit skin dexterously treated, masquerades under names that sound more alluring than the real one, while hare skin is served up in like manner. Truly the ordinary parohaser would never recognise the place in natural history of the majority of the fur bearing animals as they are now presented to us. But with all the perfec- tion that has been attained in the treatment of skins, and in the processes of dyeing and imparting lustre to the hair, a _little real fur, if only of the less expensive

kinds is really a better purchase than the most beauti- fully "faked" varieties. It wears and looks well to the last, which is more than can be said for most of the imitation sable, ermine, fox, sealskin, and chinchilla. So far,greater success has attended the imitation of sable than chinchilla or ermine,and imitation pointed fox is a fur to be avoided. The real skin has a multiplicity of white hairs, which in the imitation article are simply stuck in. These quickly fall out, and in appearance the fur is spoiled. The Leading Colour.—Tailors, dressmakers, and milliners, seem all to have • decided that brown is to be the one and only possible colour this winter. Brown chiffon, in all shades, is in great request for dance and dinner dresses, and in the streets one cannot but notice the predominance of this hue. Many of these walking gowns of the darker shades have an introduction of orange about the collar, but few complexions can stand the proximity of this startling colour, and it is best to avoid such risky attempts to give a smart and effective set off to the dress. Brown by itself is usually regarded as anything but complimentary to the great majority of women but it is only that which partakes of the dull drab shade that is open to this objection. For the brunette, the warm reddish browns are the most becom- ing, whilst the fair-haired, with a clear skin, may safely wear any shade, from the palest cafe an lait,to the soft, nut browns.

MAD GE.

RHEUMATISM CURED.

YOUR DOCTORS AND BUXTON BATHS FAILED.

BILE BEANS PROVED SUCCESSFUL.

Have you rheumatism? If so, read this Proof of what Bile Beans have done for one who used to have it.

_ Mr. Henry Poole, of Vicarage-road, Watford, is the gentleman referred to, and here is his statement : " When I reached the age of fifty-three, rheumatism settled in my legs. Day and night Ipsuffered great agony, as there was no cessation of the pain. puickly from a healthy man I was changed into a decrepit old man, able to get over no more than a few yards with the aid of crutches. I was quite infirm and deformed with pain, and I could not sit down to my meals. I had to kneel on the floor and take my food from a plate on the sofa, or other broad low piece of furniture. "I had the attendance of four different doctors during twelve months. All said the same thing—that I was a victim to muscular rheumatism. These doctors failed to remove the cause of the ailment. Afterwards I went to the University College Hospital, Gower-street, London, but nothing beneficial resulted from the treat- ment.

"The next experiment was to place myself in the hands of a physician at West Hampstead. This gentle- man was hopeful of curing me but he also failed. The last attempt to obtain relief was to go to Buxton Hospital, where I was under special treatment for four weeks.

"At Buxton, however, I met an old man who said he had derived benefit from Chas. Forde's Bile Beans. He said they were a wonderful medicine. From Buxton I went to Bournemouth. There again I heard the praises of Bile Beans as a cure for rheumatism. From Bourne- mouth I journeyed to Salisbury, my native place. Here again I was told by people who had been afflicted ,by my complaint that Bile Beans were a wonderful medicine in such cases. So while at Salisbury I gave Bile Beans a trial. - "In a comparatively short time the pain ceased. Then I became able to sit and stand more upright. Very soon I was able to get about without pain. In a few weeks I was brighter and had more confidence in myself e I continued with Bile Beans, and never had any relapse. Steadily and with increasing power I improved, and I rapidly became my old self. am thoroughly well now, and able to walk as well as ever I did. 11,1 y crutches became no longer needful after I had been taking Bile Beans for a few weeks, and I have put them away, I hope, for ever." Bile Beans are also ecure for indigestion, constipa- tion, piles, headache, female ailments, debility, flatu- lence, and all blood impurities. WILL OF MR. JAMES LYTHGOE.

Mr. James Lythgoe, of the Oddfellowsr Arms, Legh- street,Earlestown, licensed victualler, who died on the 4th September last, left estate which has been valued at £8097 4s. id. gross, including net personalty sworn at £7579 188. 4d. Probate of his will, bearing date of 14th January, 1901, has been granted to his son, Mr. Thomas Lythgoe, licensed victualler, and Miss Eliza Edwards, both of the Oddfellows' Arms, Earlestown. The testator left the whole of his estate to his son Thomas, with the exception of a sum of £5OO, which he directed should be lodged in the bank to provide a fund from which £1 per week should be paid to the said Eliza Edwards so long as she shall continue un- married, and a sum of £250 to provides fund from which the testator's daughter, Mrs. Nancy Brimelow, shall receive 10s. per week for so l long as she shall continue married to her present husband; on the death or marriage of either of these beneficiaries, the balance of their pension fund is to become the property of the Itestator's son, Thomas.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

• One never feels that summer is altogether a thing of the past until the windows of the shops display their annual selections of cosy fur garments in all the newest shapes and styles. Old fur coats which have got, out of date as to cut and are really so well worh that it is not worth while having them re-modelled can be cut up into trimmings, the largest and best pieces coming in for the rolling collar and turn back cuffs so fashionable at present, while the rest can be used up in the form of edgings to the pointed cape collars which are quite a feature of the smartest cloth and tweed costumes. It is surprising, indeed, how a little ingenuity will work Wonders with materials which to a careless eye might appear perfectly useless. For instance, I saw a really admirable idea carried out by which an old sable necklet of the straight shape that had got badly rubbed at the back of the neck so

A SMART HAT.

that it was in places quite bare, .was used te trim one of the new broad fiat-crowned hats. The lining and wadding inside were nearly all removed and the necklet was arranged in the form of a band round the crown with an old lace scarf twisted in and out of the fur, the handsome patterned ends drooping slightly over the brim at one side of the back after being knotted, while some of the hest and smallest sable tails intermingled with it in a most effective style. The hat itself was a rough surfaced white fdt and the bandeau under the brim was covered with a bow of petunia coloured velvet. The result was a very handsome and expensive looking hat at a trifling cost. My first sketch depicts a very smart felt hat. The colour is a soft grey and the felt is of the smooth kind, and in the latest form of the still fashionable "Marquise." A large bird is arranged on the crown in an unusual but very jeffective manner, and a twist of golden brown velvet is twisted Iround the crown. Nothing could be more serviceable than a hat of this style which is not too large to be inconvenient for the arrangement of a veil. I have been much stri-1 this summer

COSY COAT. by the fact that veils were so little worn, and except in the loose hanging heavily bondesed veil very few women seemed to think it necessary to protect their complexions from wind or sun. Why this should have been so I cannot say, unless it is a reflection of the cam- paign carried on by the "no hat" brigade, a set of strong minded young ladies who set custom and fashion alike at defiance and walked abroad without any protection for their heads except perhaps a sunshade. The mode certainly has a certain amount et sense in it, for there are few of us who are not aware of the benefits of plenty of sunshine and fresh air to the hair, but it also has the disadvantage of maling the exponents thereof appear out of the ordinary and therefore uncomfortably re- markable, while it almost necessitates a daily washing of the hair, and this to the women possessed of thick flowing tresses is a serious undertaking. As the weather becomes colder_ 'and more unpleasant we will, I fancy, see the death of this fad.

Very cosy indeed is the comfortable fur coat given in my second sketch. It has the advantage of being cut so that it can be worn equally well open to show a dainty front and some soft lace as tightly fastened up so as to protect both rungs and throat.VlThe sleeves are of the latest style, and it is in the much approved Russian coat mode caught in at the waist by a belt of soft kid. A PRETTY GOWN

bow. Fur coats in the early days of our mothers were things to be purchased once or perhaps twice in a lifetime, but things are very different in modern these times. A pretty and useful coat can be got at present from a couple of potluck up to any price, whereas a few years .ago nothing except ex- pensive articles could be worn by, any self- respecting woman. My last illustration depicts the charms of a really charming gown which is at once easy and comfortable to w ear and yet dressy enough looking to do duty for home dinner wear. It could be expressed in any material from silk or satin to zenana cloth or cashmere. Of course there are some Nvornen who do not care for a low necked gown such its this, but this could easily be remedied f the addition of a lace yoke either transparent or over a lining of silk. The deep lace Cape which covers the shoulders and falls yell down over the arms terminates in a senes of narrow lace frills which extend down either side of the front to the feet. These fall apart to show a front of pleated silk caught at the neck and waist by a folded band of silk secured by an ornamental buckle. Black silk or vehret makes an effective note of contrast in a pale coloured gown and • a bow to match should be worn in the hair in the manner depicted in my sketch. This is a style of gown which has proved its worth and comfort as a change from the severity of the ordinary every day style of dress. -

THE BECK INQUIRY.

AN INTERE:

./ One of the chief topics of Conversation at the moment is the Beek Inquiry. The Com- mission, which consists of Sir Richard Henn Collins, the Master of the Rolls, who is president; Sir John Edge, K.C., who was a judge in India; and Sir Spencer Walpole, who was secretary to the Post Office until recently, sits almost daily in the Royal Commission House- in Old Palace Yard, Westminster. It opened somewhat sen- sationally with a refusal by Mr. Beck, through his solicitor, Sir George Lewis, either to attend or to furnish a statement, on the ground that the Commission would not allow him to be represented by counsel and that his own English was imperfect. In opening the proceedings, the Master of the Rolls made special reference to the matter and pointed out that if one party had been represented by counsel many others would have demanded the same privilege. The inquiry, he centinued, Was now not so much for the satisfaction of Mr. Beck personally as of the nation, who were eager to find out where the mistake had come in and how such things were in the future to be avoided. As a consequence, Mr. Adolph Beck was present the second day with Sir George Lewis and received the most sympathetic treatment at the hands of the Commission AN hile he made his statement. Since then he has been present each day, asking a pertin- ent question from time to time. There has been a host of other interesting witnesses. Sir Eenelm Digby and Mr. C. E. Troup told the Home Office part of the story. Sir Robert Anderson ,and Mr. Melville Mac- naughten, with Inspectors Forrest and Ward and a crowd of lesser lights amongst the police, induding ex-Sergeant Spurrell, who candidly admitted his error in identification, represented Scotland Yard. Lord Desart, the Public Prosecutor, and Mr. Sims detailed the actions and ideas of the Treasury. Mr. Horace Avory, K.C., who prosecuted, andi Mr. Gill, K.C., who defended, both volun- teered statements, and several other lawyers gave evidence concerning their connection with the case. The Recorder, Sir Forrest Fulton, also, contrary) to all precedent or obligation, temporarily vacated the Bench for the witness table and offered the Commission statement of how the case had appeared to him, and, while speaking most sym- pathetically of Beck's unjust conviction, he frankly admitted having no recollection of prosecuting John Smith in 1877. Amongst the most interesting incidents of the inquiry were Mr. Avory's protest against Sir George Lewis's letter to Mr. G. R. Sims' articles, into which he read a reflection on himself, which, it was explained, was not implied, and the contradiction of ex-Inspector Waldock by Mr. Sims, the Treasury. solicitor, with reference to his having reported to him that Beck could not be Smith as the marks did not tally The report of the Commission will he full of interest to every Briton, and it is to be hoped that it will be followed by more adequate compensation for Mr. Beck.

THE SHELLING OF BRITISH TRAWLERS BY THE RUSSIAN FLEET.

During th'e war in the Far East there has been much unwarrantable interference with British shipping, • but it has been com- pletely eclipsed by the extraordinary incident which took place last • Friday at midnight in the North Sea.. Some 150 trawlers from Hull were peacefully fishing on the • Dogger Bank, about 200 miles from Spurn Head, when a number of men-of-war, which turned out to be the Russian Balt,.c Fleet, suddenly bore down upon them. SeaPchlights were turned upon the fishing craft, and without the slight- est warning a violent fusilade was opened upon the innocent trtwiers, carrying death and destruction among the unfortunate crews. The warships then steamed rapidly away. One of the fishing vessels—the Crane—was almost instantly sunk, its captain and one of

A STEAM TRAWLER.

the crew being killed.. The survivors, many of whom were badly wounded, were rescued. The full extent of the damage to the fishing craft and the fishermen is not yet known, but the facts have been laid before the For- eign Office and the Board of Admiralty and • the fullest investigation will take place and all the,, reparation possible demanded from the •Rii§sian Government. The general opinion is that it was not a wilful act, and it is thought in some quarters that the men-of- war mistook the trawlers for disguised Japanese vessels laying mines for the destruc- tion of the Russian Fleet, .hence the terrible blunder. In view, however, of the fact that the fishing boats were showing the usual lights. recognised' by international custom, and that they were unarmed, the outrage .seems inex- plicable. Needless to say the greatest sympathy is felt for the victims and their relatives.

THE ANNIVERSARY OF TRAFALGAR.

' The anniversary of the gr,.. t victory oft . Cape Trafalgar on October 21st, 1805, is always celebrated loyally in London, and homage is annually paid to :he memory of the great admiral mho lost his life. This year has proved no exception, and once more the lofty statue, with its Landseer lions keeping guard at the foot, -.vas c:wered with tokens, sliwking that we are not a nation of shOrt memories 'or ingratitude. Next year will be the centenary of Trafalgar, • and already plans and proposals are on foot for its proper 'celebration. It is - proposed to raise a centenary. fund of a million shillings in memory of the great admiral, to be applied for the benefit of sailors under the auspices of the British and Foreign Sailors' Society, who intend, amongst other things, to build a Sailors' Rest at King's Lynn, near Nelson's old Norfolk home. The fuird will ,be swelled by a gift from the Admiralty of ithe oak and the copper recently taken from tile H Victory" and the 11 Foudroyant,'!

A FAMOUS HEROINE.

Only 195 out of the 673 heroes returned, mosti. of them wounded, and to-day but fee' of the honoured veterans survive. Ranking with them in the public estimation comes the " veteran " Miss Florence Nightingale, the heroine of the campaign, who raised a corps of thirty-eight nurses to tend the sick and wounded in the Crimea and faced every privation and hardship on her, ex- pedition of mercy. A national testimonial of Iso,ooo was raised for her upon her return home, but, instead of applying it to private purposes, she generously used it to endow the nursing home which bears her name. Then thirty-four years of age, Miss Florence Nightingale is now eighty-four and for the last ten years has been an invalid, confined to her house .in South Street, Park Lane. Put her mind is quite clear, and she still follows anything to do with nursing with great keenness. Her name is a household word through the country, and the memory of her heroism is evergreen wherever English is spoken.

Other great anniversaries are the jubilees of the Charge of the Light Brigade at Balaclava, which took place on October 25th, 1854, and the departure of Miss Florence Nightingale with her gallant band of nurses for the Crimea three days before. Both are events of which the country is justly proud, and their fiftieth anniver- 4,. At sar y recalls them vividly to k ' ' 3 recollection. itr • . *•.. ' - - V- • '. . A THEATRICAL WEDDING.

A the;atrical wedding of more than usual interest was that of Mr. Edward Terry, the well - known actor, to Lady Harris, widow of Sir Augustus Harris, who was nicknamed " Druriolanus," after his famous theatre. The engagement had only just been made public and it was stated that the wedding would take place shortly before Christmas, prepara- tory to Mr. Terry's departure to America, whither his bride would accompany him. The ceremony, however, actually took place on Monday at the Parish Church of Barnes, where Mr. Terry resides. The wedding was a very quiet one, only a few relatives being present. The bride is the sister of Mr. Rendle, who controls the fortunes of Covent Garden, and has one daughter, Patience, who is at present acting in the provinces in Mrs. Michael Gunn's company. Mr. Edward Terry is nowadays almost better known in the provinces than in London although he owns Terry's Theatre in the Strand, where, during the last season, he produced "The' House of Burn- side." Born in 1844, this veteran actor is sixty years of age and has spent over forty of them on the stage. His father was Mr.

COVERn TRAMS.

*''A Boon to the Public, Sanctioned.

IPTAt the Council meeting on Tuesday, Alderman Smethurst moved the minutes of the Electricity and Tramways Committee.—At a meeting of this Committee a letter was read from COuncillor Peter Peacock sug- gesting the trial of halfpenny tram fares on the Long- ford route. and the running of workmen's cars after 5 p.m. It was resolved" That the manager be instructed to run workmen's cars in accordance with the above suggestion." A copy of a report prepared by the Tram- ways Manager on roof coverings for cars was supplied to each member of the committee, and the considera- tion thereof deferred to the next meeting.—lt was resolved that the manager be instructed to report as to the advisability of running overlapping stages through- out the system; and the proposal to charge a halfpenny fare on the Longford route. A letter was read from the Postmaster asking that special facilities should be given for postmen to travel On the cars at a reduced fare so as to expedite the postal service in the suburbs, , and after discussion it was resolved, that the Town • Clerk be instructed to inform the Postmaster that the committee regret they cannot see their way to comply ' with his request. At a. meeting on the 21st October it was resolved that an order be placed with Messrs. Milner, Voss and Co., of Birkenhead, to supply four top covers for tramcars at £BO each, including the cost of new trolley poles. Alderman SMETHURST in proposing the acceptance of the above minutes said the committee had had a special meeting and sanctioned the ordering of 4 cover-tops for tramcars at each. The experience of other towns , was that the introduction of covered cars increased the ' revenue. They had been to Birkenhead and other places * and he thought they had got the best type of cover-top. A VOICE: Have it as a smoker. The MAYOR : They can smoke as much as they like. Councillor WILKINSON seconded.

Councillor TINNION asked where the money was coming from to pay for these covers. it was not fair to the officials that committees should spend money which would have to be added to the capital account without the sanction of the Local Government Board. The officials would have to go before the Board and make excuses and it was not fair to them. The com- mittee had neither the money to pay for these covers nor the power to borrow it. Councillor Ev.txs said he was inclined to take the matter even further. Here was a case in which four of these covershad already been ordered before the sanc- tion of the Council had been obtained.

I The MAYOR: The orders are given subject to the confirmation of the minutes by the Council. I Councillor EVANS : I will move an amendment that this minute be not confirmed. I The MAYOR: The covers cannot be delivered for a month and the Local Government Board will be applied to before then. I Councillor EVANS: Money should not be spent in this way without the sanction of the Council. Councillor PEMBERTON seconded the amendment. He was a member of the committee but he did not get sufficient notice for the special meeting of the com- mittee. He had only about five hours' notice and he was unable to attend the meeting. Councillor WILONSON said the action of the com- mittee was perfectly justifiable. There was no cheaper or better cover-deck on the market than this. There was nothing really wrong in the action they had taken. At 4 percent. the cost per annum of the capital expen- diture would only he £l2, and they would get both " downstairs "and " upstairs " full. Councillor STOTT supported the principle of no goods being ordered before the sanction of the .Council had been obtained. Some of the committees needed teach- ing their place. Rethought the Tramways Committee was to blame for not taking the matter up earlier than the beginning of October. Alderman SUTTON supported the committee because he thought the covers were necessary for the protection of the publi3. Alderman PIERPOINT said that though he had been unable to attend the meeting of the committee their action had his warmest sympathy. Although the principle was perhaps wrong, it was not the first time they had had a sermon about the spending of £lOO when thousands had been passed without a word. This was a matter of urgency. Councillor THORNTON said they were all agreed upon the question of principle, but seeing the urgency of the matter he should vote for the minutes. Councillor TINNION : If the committee is going to apply immediately for the money I will vote for the expenditure, but not unless sanction is obtained. Councillor BURTON asked if there would be any delay caused by adopting the amendment ? (Cries of "Yes.") "Then I shall vote for the minutes on the question of urgency,"he added. Alderman S3IETHURST (to the objectors) : Some of you gentlemen pose as the workingman's friend, yet you would sooner he rode on a wet seat. (Laughter.) Councillor EVANS: I rise to a point of order ! The MAYOR: Mr. James Evans will withdraw that he is the workingmen's friend. (Laughter.) Councillor EVANS: He is talking entirely away from the amendment.

Alderman SMETHURST said they had been so pressed to get these covers that they were anxious to avoid any delay. He asked them, on behalf of the travelling public, to pass the minutes. There ought to be accommodation for a man to go on top and smoke his pipe.

'Councillor TINNION : His cigar! (Loud laughter in which Alderman Smethurst joined). A vote was then taken and the amendment was lost by 19 votes to 7. The minutes were then confirmed.

SHIP CANAL COMPANY SUED. Warrington Cab Driver's Claim for Damages.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday,before his •Honour Judge Bo wen Rowlands, K.C., William Goodenough, Wharf-street, brought an action against the Manchester Ship Canal Company, to recover £2 for damages to a waggonette caused by the negligence of the company in having a swing bridge below the level of the highway. Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff, Mr. Newman, Manchester, appearing for the defendant company. Mr. Browne explained that Mr. Goodenough had on a former occasion recovered damages from the Man- chester Ship Canal Company, for an accident to a waggonette caused in exactly the same way. He understood that the defendants were under the impression that the waggonette, the damages to which formed the subject of that action, was the one which was damaged on a former occasion. That was not so as the waggonette had been hired from a respectable firm of job masters in the town Mr. Goodenough was a charac- ter who was well known in Warrington, who made a living by driving people to different shows on the out- skirts of the town. On the date in question,Mr. Good- enough was engaged bringing persons to and from Walton Show, having on the way to cross over the Stag bridge over the Ship Canal. The swing bridges across the canal when turned back were not exactly the same level as the roadway and had to be raised to that level by machinery operated within a cabin. On tile date in question the bridge had evidently not been raised after being turned as when Mr. Goodenough drove on to it he felt a lump. He drove on to the crossings where he put down his passengers. He then examined the waggonette when he found the body was damaged and the springs out of order. He immediately drove back to the swing bridge and showed tha damage to Mr. Campbell, the man in charge. He then made a more thorough examination of the waggonette, and found it was in such a serious condition that it could not be used with safety. He accordingly returned it to the person from whom he hired it. The waggonette, which was in good condition when taken out, was repaired at a cost of gl 4s. 6d. The balance of the claim was made up by loss of business. Mr. Newman cOntended that plaintiff had no ground for an action. The only person who could bring an action against the Ship canal Company was the owner of the damaged waggonette. The owners of the waggonette could not recover from Mr. Goodenough provided he had exeraised ressonablacare whilst driving. His Honour held with Mr. Newman, on the point with regard to the damage to the waggonette. Mr. Browne then amended his claim and sought to recover damages for loss at business resultant upon the accident.

Mr. Newman said he had no objection to Mr. Good- enough being compensated and offered the sum of 15s. This sum Mr. Browne agreed to accept and with the consent of his Honour withdrew the claim.

BISHOP OF HEREFORD AT BARLESTOWS.

On Monday evening the annual reception given by Colonel R. Pilkington M.P., and Mrs.Pilkington to the members and friends Of the Earlestown branch of the Y.M.C.A. was held at the Town Hall, there being a very large gathering which included the Bishop of Hereford (Dr. Percival) and leaders in the Y.M.C.A. institution.—The Bishop of Hereford said that one of the most unfortunate things in the life of any com- munity was sectarian jealousy and antagonism. He had just returned from Boston, U.S.A., and what struck him most was the absolutely friendly way in which all denominations of Christians worked together, despite the fact that the men who founded Boston, though earnestly religious, were the most intolerant people on earth. He cited instances of the absence of sectarian bitterness and division ther6, and said that in the town of Manchester, Mass., the Protestants contributed to the erection of a Roman Catholic church, but when the Protestants were building a church the [Lamm Catholic Archbishop said it was contrary to his faith to support the building of a heretical church, vet he would give them a hundred dollars towards the cost of palling the old one down. (Applause.) He had hoped a few years ago th :t the feeling of union between the Nonconformist bodies and the Church would have grown stronger, but it had a check, though he hooad to oatlive that check, and to see the spirit of moral ~-eadwill growing apace. The united services of the Y.N1.0 A. would do great wark in Ii at respect.

DEATH OF MR. EDWARD

PICKAVANCE,

Another well-known Warrington tradesman has passed away in the person of Mr. Edward Pickavance, who since 1870 had carried on the business of a leather mer- chant in Bridge-street. Two years ago Mr. Pickavance was seized with a serious illness whilst on a visit to London, from which he never properly recovered. About three months back he was again taken ill and was confined to his bed. Small hopes of his ultimate recovery were entertained, and he passed away early on Saturday morning. The deceased gentleman, who was in his 66th year, came to Warrington from St. Helans in 1870, and founded the leather business which for a number of years was carried on under the title of "Abel and Pickavance." Ten years ago the partnership was dissolved and since then the business has been carried on under the name of "Picka- vance and Son." In politics he was a staunch Liberal, and in his earlier years took an active interest in the work af the Warrington Liberal Association. He 'rasa regular worshipper at Wycliffe Congregational Church pnd was very closely identified with the Sunday School connected with that place of worship, conducting a class for over 30 years, in fact by his death both the church and Sunday school have lost one of theie strongest supporters. He leaves one son and four daughters, and a large elide of friends to mourn his loss.

SONGS OF AGONY.

Delirious with Sciatica, this Man Finally Collapsed. Dr. Willians' Pink Pills made him an Active Worker again. "I am toldithat when delirious with pain I actually sang songs," said Frederick Friday, and those who have suffered from Sciatica know what he endured. "For sears," he said to a "Merthyr Express" repo- " I was a martyr to • Sciatica, and suffered more 111P* than I can tell you." Mr. Friday put his hand to his hip with a sigh of relief.
