There is only too THE CRiSIS, much reason to

believe that the Russian Government is unwilling to make the only reparation that can be accepted for the acts of the flcating Bedlam which calls itself the Baltic Fleet. The 11. D3IIRAL, who is at Vigo, reports that his battleships have already been knocking themselves to pieces. Two of them, he says, are "damaged." Perhaps he will tell the world that the damage was inflicted by the Japanese torpedo boats he saw among the trawlers in the North Sea. No further proof, however, is needed to establish 'tis htate of mind, and the responsibility which rests upon his employers. They are said to be disposed to make an apology and a money payment, but not to discover the criminals, or punish the crime The feAling at St. Petersburg is expressed very succinctly by the correspondent of the "Daily Telegraph." "The Government," say the naval officials in that capital, "is absolutely certain that England will never go to war for a couple of fishermen, and, that being so, Russia will refuse to punish the officers, because by doing so she would brand-mark her Navy with enduring infamy, by implicitly stigmatising not one or two officers, but the whole officers' corps, as nervous, cruel, and cowardly." That stigma is inflicted already on the person or persons responsible for wilful murder on the high seas. The only way to prevent the stigma from extending to Russia hehelf is to punish the offenders. A Government which refuses to do that in the face of universal opinion outside

MEM

===== 0002601_19041029_art0073.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
Oburatton.

===== 0002601_19041029_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
its own borders will stamp itself as uncivilised,and as an open foe to the comity of nations. We have ' been told that it is not fair to indict the Rubsian Government and the Russian nation for this 'infamous business. The Russian nation has no more to do with it than that nation had to do with the origin of the war with Japan. But the Russian Government—if it rAfuses to make just amends for what Lord ROSEBERY calls "this unspeakable outrage," for what Sir HENRY CAMPRELL-BANNER- mAN calls " this unparalleled and cruel outrage" and" this act of atrocity "—will stand convicted as the accomplice of its barbarous agents, and the blood, not of two poor inoffensive fishermen alone, but of two nations, will be on its head. We say that any Government with a just sense of its responsibility to neutrals would at once have re-called its ADMIRAL, and given a pledge to mete out to him the penalty he deserves. Reparation is needed, not "explanation." It is only too pro- bable that the brazen language of the Russian Press exactly represents what this officer is said to call his "conscience-" "The North Sea inci- dent," he is reported to have stated at Vigo, "was inevitable. He acted in accordance with his conscience, with the object of preventing the destruction of his squadron. Before leaving Libau he had made known his intention of attack- ing any ship which approached the Fleet. If this is also the " conscience " of the Russian Government, we see no alternative to the policy ,of prohibiting any further adventures on the part of the floating Bedlam. Without the permission of the British Navy ic cannot pursue its voyage to the Far East. Should that per- mission be refused, and should the conscientious Russian ADMIRAL persist, then he and his Baltic Fleet will probably be seen no izore. We pre- sume that, in such an event, the Black Sea Fleet would come out of the Black Sea and subside to the bottom of the Mediterranean. The maritime power of Russia would thed cease to exist, and a little reflection ought to show the CZAR and his advisers that they are not in a position just now to attack India. All these reflections, we hope will operate for the maintenance of peace between Russia and England.

[Since writing the above we have learned that an inquiry is to be held at Figo,where the Russian fleet will remain for 20 days. England's demands are likely to be acceeded to—at any rate nothing less will satisfy or appease the indignant British nation.—En. "Ex."]

That public interest in the municipal trading question has not waned was evidenced by the huge attendance at Parr Hall on Monday even- ing when the Non-Traders gathered together to roview the Inquiry reports. The people of War- rington have waited impatiently and eagerly for those reports, and since their publication in the "Examiner"on Saturday they have formed the chief topic of conversation at chance meetings and in all places "where men do congregate."ln our last issue we had no opportunity of dealing with the find- ings of the Commissioners or the principles in- volved, at all comprehensively, but the lapse of seven days has removed the difficulty. The two reports are,as we need hardly po'nt out,absolutely contradictory. This was to be expected, seeing that the Commission was composed of gentlemen interested either in proving or disproving certain allegations, and not competent to produce an im- partial or unanimous verdict. The futility of two reports is obvious now that they are issued. Public opinion still remains divided, but there is,we think a general trend of thought towards the point of view of the Non-Traders&

Our own opinion on the result of the inquiry is firm and unalterable. The evidence estabhshes strongly the contention that it is against the public interest for men who are members of the Council to trade with the Corporation through limited companies. We emphasize the verb "to trade" for this reason: that it would be impossible to charge a man with trading who held only £lOO worth of shares in a company with £lOO,OOO capital. No line can very well be drawn beyond which a man must not go in his proportion of share-holding before he can be called a trader through any particular z..ornpany, but if it is made clear that the Council and the ratepayers will not tolera.e trading which is sheltered only by the legal cloak of the limited liability Act there will be no necessity for any such line of demarcation. Tne report of the Non-Trading Commissioners proves conclusively that there has been trading of this description, and in exposing it both in its direct and indirect forms they have performed a valuable public service. We regret very much to note that the Corporation Commis- sioners by a" remarkable piece of sophistry "—to use their own words—argue that such trading is right. They appear to forget that public men should be above snspic:o. n. The cold, clear light of criticism is ever beating it the representa- tives of the ratepayers, and t is due to themselves as much as to those they represent,that they should always:be mindful of the example they Bet and of the solemn trust reposed in them.

The Town Council, at its meeting on Tuesday did not,fortunately, attempt to discuss the reports though several members showed an unmistakable disposition to do so. It was even suggested that a special night should be set apart for the purpose or that Parr Hall might be engaged for the Coun- cil representatives to have their fling, but nothing has come of these suggestions as yet. We there- fore hasten to ask why the Council should discuss the reports at all ? They would never come to agreement if they talked all night, and they would not enlighten the ratepayers either. The discus- sion of the reports is not a matter for the Town Council as a council, but for the townspeople only. All the Council is called upon to do is to accept the reports and cause them to be printed in the minutes. It would be rank bad policy for any members of the Council to take Parr Hall and attempt at this late hour to argue the pros and cons of municipal trading. The commissioners have done that effectively, and the verdict can be safely left with the public.

1 ' There were several angry scenes during the ' course of the meeting, and a stranger would prob- ably have concluded that the members were the least decorous body of municipal administrators he had ever mot with. Squabbles are the I order of the evening when our Town Council meets I and there seems, unfortunately, little hope of any improvement. The trade union wages and penalty clauses in Corporation contracts were the cause of , the most serious revolt against the usages of debate on Tuesday. Councillor Stott made a third attempt to get a penalty clause added to those he has already been instrumental in placing on the stand- ing orders, but he failed hopelessly. A month or two back it is probable that the proposition would have been accepted,for the Council then looked upon the suggestions from the Trades Council as harm- less and reasonable. But, in the meantime,as the working of the new orders has be 3ome better understood, opinion has undergone a considerable change, and we shall probably be not far from the mark when we predict the rescinding of the whole of the new standing orders relating to contracts at an early date. Their operation would be harmful in many respects, and they by no means answer to the wishes of the majority. Councillor Stott exposed his hand to some extent when he was led into alluding to the printing trade, which is the first to be affected by the new contract clauses. Like other trade unionists he is opposed to female labour and he made it obvi- ous that the employment of girls on the three local newspapers was one of the things he objected to. They, their fathers, or. their brothers, will hardly welcome his intervention. It is a childish notion to suppose that women are to be debarred from doing work for which they are better adapted

===== 0002601_19041029_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
than 'men, merely because they are women, an the trade unionists might as well run with their heads against the nearest wall as adopt this atti- tude in the twentieth century. We would like to put Councillor Stott right on one point in particu- lar. The employment of women does not "bring men on the streets." It increases employment. Were women not available for the class of work under notice,typesgtting machinery would be used to the exclusion largely of both sexes. For a pro- fessed labour man, Councillor Stott holds some peculiarly conservative and narrow views.

The thousands of tram-users it& Warrington will be delighted to learn that the Council has sanc- tioned the:purchase of four cover-tops for the cars. This is an instalment of what we hope will become a general scheme. There is nothing more annoying —among minor annoyances—than to be driven on the exposed top of a tram in bad weather with nothing to sit on but a dripping seat. Covered tops are a better solution of the difficulty than patent reversible seats. We know from experi- ence how convenient and popular two-deckers are in other towns. To the smoker they are an inestimable boon, and why should not he be con- sidered? He is nurnerons enough. Though it violated a principle to save time in the rnatter,the Tramways Committee is deserving of thanks for its anterprise and consideration. We shall be having contracts and half-penny stages and the millenium next !

The November elections will take place on Tuesday. Only a week ag® it was generally ex- pected that there would be contests in the majority of the Wards, but the belligerents have dwindled in number so much that only three wards will be put to the trouble of balloting for their representa- tives. Only one of the fights is on political lines, the other two being .due to the presence in the field of independent candidates. So far as any arrangement between the leaders of the two political parties is concerned the compact may be said to be dead, but there is still a good deal of quiet arranging going on between the Liberal and Conservative election officials. Probably something of the kind is unavoidable, but the less there is and the better. For our part we welcome the presence of independent candidates. They keep alive the electors' interest in the government of the town, and help to counteract that apathy which is largely responsible for the management of the town's affairs being so unsatisfactory.

The only political struggle is in Howley Ward, where Mr. S. Davies, the retiring Councillor, is being opposed by Mr. W. Clegg, a well-known Liberal tradesman. A keen fight seems certain, with slight odds on Mr. Clegg, who is a popular and capable candidate. In Bewsey Ward there is an intetesting contest proceeding between Coun- cillor Rogers and Mr. W. Skelland dne of the oldest and best respected residents in the district. Councillor Rogers has always been looked upon as:a strong candidate but seeing that he has left the neigbourhood, the chances are that Bewsey people will prefer the man on the spot in the person of Mr. Skelland. If only to give him an opportunity of obtaining redress for the grievance which the Ragged School has against the Water Committee we hope Bewsey electors will vote for Mr. Skelland. In Latchford Ward, Councillor Luke Ellison and Mr. W. Benson are opponents. Both live in the ward and both profess Liberal principles so that the voters will hav, some difficulty in making their choice. Mr. Benson is a non-trader alto) and this fact may have its infivence on the contest, seeing that the ward plumped so solidly for Councillor Pemberton on this question two years ago.

The accommodation difficulty was once more the theme of discussion at Monday evening's meeting of the Education Committee, and the feeling was freely expressed that there has been unnecessary dilatoriness in embarking upon the erection of new schools. Although the authority has been in existence eighteen months, some con- siderable time must elapse before the foundation of the first a iditional school is laid. Allowance must, of course, be made for the difficulty ex- perienced in obtaining sites at a reasonable figure, but there appears to be a great deal of justification for the contention that the work might have been pushed forward much more rapidly. Now, at any rate, there is a clearer p_os- pect of active measures being taken, for the Com- mittee has sanctioned the purchase of the site for the new" Bolton "schools at Latchford, The decision to invite all local architects to send in competitive designE is to be commended. We would again express the hope that in selecting the successful design the committee will not be disposed to spend money needlessly on elaborate ornamentation. The provision of adequate accom- modation throughout the borough is likely to be a heavy drain upon the public purse, and therefore utility and the health and comfort of the scholars should be the main considerations.

The public health should be a matter of thought- ful concern to every intelligent member of the community,aud it is regrettable that comparatively few townspeople availed themselves of the oppor- tunity of hearing Professor W. R. Smith's lecture at the Parr Hall on the causes and prevention of infectious diseases. Whether the disappointing attendance was due to indifference or over- confidence on so important a subject it would be difficult to say, but the fact remains that the Health Committee's effort in the interest of the populace deserved much more hearty appreciation and support. The lectuee was full of sound, homely hints and served to dispel some of the popular but erroneous ideas concerning the more familiar diseases. We would draw special attention to the stress which the lecturer laid upon the need for light and frAsh air in the homes of the people. This naturally leads one to the fringe of the wider question of housing refurm, for how can the dwellers in dismal, ill- lighted hovels be expected to possess the inestimable blessing of good health? The authorities will, we feel sure, have the support of the vast majority of townspeople in any efforts they put forth to secure better sanitation in congested areas. M.anwhile we would point out that it is due from the public to take more general advantage of the remainder of the course of lectures.

STOCKTON HEATH.

MEETING OF THE PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE. The monthly meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee was held in the National schools on Thursday evening, when there were present—Messrs. Pilling (presiding), Whitley, Warburton, Bouchier, Williams, Hallows Mounfield, T. R. Westbrook, Mclntyre,H. J. Westbrook,Welsby and Pearson,together with Messrs. G. F. Ashton (clerk), Swinton (surveyor), and Farrington (inspector).

===== 0002601_19041029_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
WALTON SEWAGE SCHEME.

The Clerk laid before the meeting plans for the Jiro- posed sewage scheme for Walton Inferior. He explained. that at present the Walton Parish Council did not pro- pose to join the Stockton Heath sewer, but proposed to carry an additional sewer along Ellesmere-road to join the sewage syphon under the canal. The scheme, ex- plained the clerk, would cost £1619. Mr. H. J.Westbrook moved that the plan be approved provided that the rights of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee, with regard to the first claim upon the Canal syphon were safeguarded. Mr. Pearson seconded and the proposal was carried. Mr. Hallows moved that it be a recommendation to the District Council that the Walton sewer be joined to Stockton Heath sewer, subject to a satisfactory, arrangement being arrived at. The construction of a second sewer would mean more manholes, and the resi- dents in Ellesmere-road were opposed to this on account of the nuisance which would be caused by smells from the manholes.

Mr. Mounield remarked that no nuisance would arise from the manholes, as the Walton Parish Council were intending to provide ventilating shafts. Mr. Hallows' proposal was carried. LIGHTING. _ _ _ _

Mr. Whitley laid before iiie Meeting some informa- tion he had obtained with regard to the cost of lighting the streets under the present system of extinguishing the lamps at midnight, as compared with the cost of allowing the lamps to remain lighted throughout the I night. f they adopted the last-named system it would mean an additional cost of 10s. per lamp, or-q sum in total equal to one penny in the £ on the rates. The matter was left in the hands of the Lighting Committee.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour, Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C., Harry Herbert Raley, Penketh, sought through his father to recover from John Rotherham, Penketh, damages for injuries caused by being bitten by the defendant's dog.—Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff, the defendant appearing in person. The facts briefly stated were that on 20th May of this year the plaintiff was passing the defendant's house in -the rear when the defendant's dog rushed out and bit him on the right leg. Plaintiff had to be medically attended for a month. Defendant had been previously warned to keep the dog fastened up as it had bitten other people. His Honour gave judgment for the Paintiff for 255. with costs. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63, SANKEY STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Claim against an Earlestown Accountant. GREAT SALE OF BOOTS AND SHOES 110 W on UNDER THE CLOCK.—Advt. Warrington Crimean Veteran's Death. Another of the veterans who in their early days took part in the Crimean campaign, passed away on Thursday, in the person of Sergeant W. Bodle, who lived in Wilderspool-road. Mr. Bodle who was in his 75th year, aid who had been ailing for some time, joined the Royal Artillery in 1848, obtaining his dis- charge in 1870. While connected with the array he served throughout the whole of the campaign in the Crimea, being in the whole or the big engagements including Alma and Sebastopol, and witnessing the memorable charge of the Light Brigade. During the whole of the time he was in the Crimea he was never on the sick or injured list. Returning home he was the first man to be employed by the-late Mr. Thomas Fletcher, the founder of the firm of Messr.i. Fletcher Russell and Company, and during the remainder of his life he was actively connected with the film. Mr. Bodle -was extremely well known locally, and in addi- tion to his wife and family, leaves a large circle of friends to mourn his loss Christian Endeavour Convention. The tenth autumnal Convention of the Warrington and District Christian Endeavour Union Was held on Saturday, in the Primitive Methodist Chapel at Lymm. The pretty locality evidently proved an attraction as more sat down to tea than at any previous Convention, endeavourers coming from near and far. The, after- noon conference was preceded by a devotional meet- ing which was conducted by the Rev. S. R. Woodall, pastor of the Chapel. Mr,gk-Pitchford of Chester presided over the conference and delivered an address on "The new era of Hope." Papers were read by Miss Sherrington of Newton-le-Willows on "The defects of Christian Endeavour Societies and how to remedy," by Mr. J. Hampson, of Warrington, on "The Zndeavour and :the modern use of Sunday," and by Mr. C. Rawlings of Earlestown on "The increase campaign." The discussion which followed was so well sustained that the shades of evening 'were falling before the party proceeded to tea.At the close of tea an open-air meeting was held at the foot of the historical cross of Lymm. The chapel was well-filled at the public-meeting in the evening. The president of the Union . Rev. J. S. Hughes presided and delivered an address on "The coming revival." Addresses were also delivered by Rev. F. Vaughan, Warrington, and by Mr. H. Brimelow of Boltbn. A brief Consecration service brought to a close what was termed by many "the best Convention ever held by. the -Union.' Mention must be made of the beautiful singing of Miss Leigh-Warbutton and Mr. Brazendale, whose solos and duets added much to the pleasure and. profit of the meetings.

Church Society Conversazione. The annual conversazione in connection with the Warrington Church Society attracted a large gather- ing to the Kendrick-street schools on Wednesday evening. The Rev. Morley Stevenson, president of the Society, occupied the chair, and at the outset ex- plained the great need which there was for the exis- tence of the Society, in order to correct many wrong ideas which people entertained with regard to the Church of England. He spoke of the retirement of the Rector from the presidency of the Society, after having held that office for 12 years, and expressed the hope that he would soon see his way to again accept office. ("Hear, hear.)—Mr. Torr, Lincoln addressed the meeting on the subject of "Our oppor- tunity? The Ch-arch of England had he said, the opportunity of occupying a position which neither the Roman Church or the Free Churches had occupied or had the opportunity to occupy. That was because the Church of England was the most comprehensive church in the world. If she wa to realize that position which she ought to occupy, they would, however, have to recognize that it was not Christianity or Catholicity to narrow down the Church and exclude those who did not see eye to eye with them. (Applause). Mr. Torr was heartily thanked for his address, on the motion of Mr. Gibson, seconded by the Rector. During the evening refreshments were handed round, and songs were given by Mrs. C. Lesters and Mr. Potts. Miss M. Fairdough acted as acconipanist.

SL Clement's Mission.

The Rev. A. S. Cameron preaching at. St. Clement's Mission on Sunday evening, took for his subject the series of letters which are appearing in a London news- paper on the question of" Do. we believe ?" After re- viewing the letters, the rev, gentleman spoke of the large amount of literature which was being published condemning the teaching of the Bible. In conclusion, he said that it was not only necessary to believe, but it was the duty of all Christians Ito prove by their examples and lives that belief in Christ made good and honourable citizens.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church.

The anniversary services to be held at this church to- morrow and Mondav are of unusual interest. The Rev. Dr. Beet, an old Warrington Wesleyan minister who has risen to great eminence in the world of religious thought, will conduct the services throughout Sunday, and on Monday at 7.30 will lecture on 'The Old Testa- ment and Modern Thought," a subject which is com- manding very earnest attention at the present day. On Sunday afternoon at 2.30 Dr. Beet will hold a Con- ference at which he will answer questions on diffi- culties of belief. Dr. Beet wishes that the questions shall be sent up to him in writing, but will allow other questions if there is time sufficient for such a method of questioning. Dr. Beet is not only a deep thinker, but also an acute reasoner and an exceedingly clear speaker. Services of special attractiveness are there- fore confidently expected.

===== 0002601_19041029_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P,S.A. Society.

There was a very good attendance at the meeting on Sunday. The secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) presided and urged every member to make up his mind to bring one new member during the year. He also welcomed five new members, making a total of 545. Mr. Bell of Manchester, spoke upon "Duty" and gave some very good illustrations of the many phases of doing one's duty. He asked the young men present to attend well to their duty of keeping the doors shut to all evil ways, evil communications, and evil companions, and at all times, to honour their parents. Rev. John Yonge led the meeting in prayer. Mr. John Willett jun., sang "The Beautiful City," /(the brethren joining in the chorus), and "The Man of Sorrows." Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. An open meeting of the above society will be held to-morrow, and will be presided over by Mr. E. W. Bindloss. An address will be given by Mr. J. R. Wolstencroft (Swinton). Messrs. Crosfield's prize band will accompany the hymns and will give special selections, including the test pisee in the recent con- test won by them at the Crystal Palace.

Epochs in the History of the English Bible.

The Rev. J .F. Matthews lectured on this subject the Latchford Baptist Church on Thursday evening. His remarks were devoted mainly to the work of William Tyndale, who, both as a controversialist, scholar, preacher, and translater, took a foremost place among our prominent reformers. In reply to a contro- versialist, who had asserted that "we had better be without God's law than the Pope's," he replied, "I defy the Pope and all his laws ; and if God spare me I will one day make the boy who drives the plough inEng- land to know more of Scripture than'thePope'does."The lecturer dealt with the great work which Tyndale under- took, in spite of Roman persecution, of translating and printing the New Testament. Through the treachery of Henry TPhillips, an English clergyman Tyndale was apprehended, and confined to the Castle of Vilvorde, near Antwerp, whence in 1536 he was taken to the stake. He was strangled and then burnt. His last prayer was "Lord open the King of England's eyes." By the kind permission of Mr. Madeley, chief librarian, the lecturer exhibited a copy of Wycliffe's New Testa- ment, printed in 1810, and also a copy of the Breeches Bible dated 1615, loaned.by a friend.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 29, 1904.

THE MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

THREE CONTESTS.

Tuesday next will witness the annual municipal elections, but in Warrington there will be only three contests. List week-end it was freely rumoured that Mr. Henry Roberts intended to seek the suffrages of the electors, and in order that the feeling on the non- trading question might he tested the supporters of Mr. James Dodds nominated him for nl fewer than seven wards. Mr. Rlberts, however, was not nominated and Mr. Dodds withdrew his candidature in every ward. The result is that only the electors of Bewsey, Howley and Latchford Wards will have an opportunity of recording their votes. Below is the complete list of candidates:— Town Hall Ward Councillor Forshaw (C.) Whitecross Ward Councillor Brough (L.) Bewsey Ward Councillor Rogers (Ind.) Mr. James Skelland (Ind.) Orford Ward Councillor Wilkinson (L.) St. John's Ward Mr. J. Fulton Smith (C.) Fairfield Ward ' Councillor Duncan (L.) Howley Ward Councillor Davies (C.) Mr. W. Clog?. (L.) St. Austin's Ward Councillor Burton (L.) Latchford Ward Mr. W. Benson (N.T.)

It will be seen that Councillor H. Thornton decided not to seek re-election for Sr,. .Tohn's W trd and Mr. J. Fulton Smith, the Conservative candidate, is returned unopposed.An interesting contest is in progress in Bewsey Ward where Mr. James Skelland. the respected founder and superintendentlof the Ragged School,has entered the lists against Councillor William Rogers, the retiring representative. Both candidates describe them- selves as "Independents." and in his address, which will be found in another column, Mr. Skelland says he is actuated solely by a desire for the welfare of the town and its inhabitants more especially those of the poorer class. "I have noticed with concern" he says "the prolonged bad trade and the resultant distress which becomes more petite each winter. I would therefore support any wise and practical measures which might be brought for- ward to aid the unemployed. lam of opinion that it is the duty of each community to study the welfare of its poor children, believing that care for the body and ' good influences on the mind of the young are powerful factors for good on the men and women of the future." Mr. Skelland alludes to his deep interest in the i Ragged school and points out that through its agency thousands of children have been fed and clothed and re- ceived good Christian influences, and many have been rescued from despair and crime and are to-day respect- able law-abiding citizens. He expresses himself in favour of free breakfasts for school children (baring ' periods of local distress,and states that scores of children ' in Warrington often went to school last winter without breakfast. He also advocates free baths for children as being a sanitary hecessity and declares himself a strong supporter of the fair wage clause in Corporation con- tracts anything that would tend to the better housing of the working classes; half-penny stages on the trams andia reduction in the price of gas" I would oppose" he adds "all extravagant and unnecessary expenditure such as has been going on for some years, and which has brought our rates rapidly to the present alarming figure, to the detriment of local industries and to the employment of our workmen." In Howley Ward the retiring representative, Coun- cillor S. Davies, is being opposed by Mr. W. Clegg who had been actively conducting his candidature during the past few days. 'He is the nominee of the Liberal Ward Committee and in his address says he is fully convinced that the time has arrived when a new bridge should be erected to connect the Howley and Latchford Wards. He is in favour of the abolition of gas meter rents and the utilizing of a large proportion of the gas profits in reducing the price to consumers. He promises to sup- port the fair wage clause in Corporation contracts and is convinced that" a judicious publicity of all Corpora- tion trading transactions would be a safeguard against all questionable dealings." Thelonly Non-Trading candidate is Mr. William Benson who comes forward in Latchford Ward in opposition to Councillor Luke Ellison who is again standing at the request of the Liberal Ward Committee. Mr. Benson while stating that he would do his utmost to prevent all useless expense, says he will be prepared to favourably consider any really necessary improvement. He thinks no one should benefit from membership of a public body; also that there should be impartial treatment of all tenders for public work. He is in sympathy with a strict observance of the fair wage clause. POLLING STATIONS AND OFFICIALS.

THE HOWLEY WARD CONTEST.

Meeting in Support of Mr. Clegg's Candid3tura

Last eve ling a meeting in furtherance of NIL-. W. Clegg's candidature in Howley W trd was held in the National Schools, Church-street, Mr. W. H. Sherburn presiding over a good attendance. The CHAIRMA.N pointed out that municipal elections were really of more importance to the working classes than Parliamentary contests, as they affected the homes of the people. It was therefore essential that. they should have good representatives In the Council. Mr. Clegg would, he was sure, be a useful member of the Council, and as a resident in the ward he would be able to serve their interest with crelit. (Applause).

Mr. C. JA.OKSON HOLMES said Mr. dlegg was a very energetic man and the fact that he was a successful business man should be arecommen dation.

------- _ _ Mr. CLEGG devoted the major part of his speech to a criticism of his opponent's address. He fully agreed with Councilor Davies as to the desirability of keeping down the rates but he would point out that during Councillor Davies' period of service in the council the rates had increased. If Councillor Davies was such an ardent advocate af economy why did he recently vote for an increase of salary of £lOO to one of the Corpora- tion officials. He also supported the expenditure of £8,600 on building operations at the Houghton Green waterworks. Councillor Davies in his address said that as a member of the Gas Committee he would have special opportunities to bring about a reduction in the price of gas, but he had not fully availed himself of those opportunities in the past, as there had been very few reductions during his period of office. Mr. Davies, he contended, had done very little to push on improvements in the ward. Some of the streets were badly lighted, whilst the road leading to H.arboard- street was positively dangerous after dark. Mr. Clegg assured the meeting that if returned to the Council he would advocate the erection of a new bridge to connect the Howley and Latchford Wards. Such a bridge would be a convenience to the residents, and its erection would provide work for the unemployed. Gas profits, he considered, would be better used in reducing the price to the consumer than in relieving the rates. If elected he would do all he could for the efficient light- ing and sanitation of the ward. Mr. JOSEPH HOWES delivered an interesting address on municipal government, referring more particularly tolthe necessity for housing reform. A vote of confidence in 'the candidate was accorded on the proposition of Mr. VOSE.

TOWN HALL WARD BYE-ELECTION.

The election to fill the casual vacancy in Town Hall Ward caused by the retirement of Councillor Dodds will take place on Tuesday week. Nominations must be received not later than Monday next at five o'clock. At a meeting of the Conservative Ward Committee held on Thursday under the presidency of Councillor Forshaw, it was unanimously decided to invite Mr. A. J. Jolley to contest the ward. Mr. Jolley was present and having thanked the meeting for the invitation con- sented to stand. He intimated that his views on the Non-Trading question had not undergone any change since his defeat in Fairfield Ward two years ago. We hear also that Mr. George Clay and Councillor Thornton may be nominated for the same seat.

The POstmaster-General, replying to communications as to annoyance caused to the public by surcharges on postcards exceeding the prescribed limit of .size, 51 inches in length and 31 inches in width, has given in- structions that for six months postcards which exceed the prescribed dimensions by not more than 1-16 of an inch, and are otherwise in conformity with the regula- tions, shall be allowed to pass without challenge. On the arrival of a Midland train at Wigston Leicestershire, a ticket collector found a passenger without a ticket. When taken to the stationmaster's office it was found that the passenger was a woman in man's clothes, and she pulled off a light brown moustache which she was wearing. She explained that her own clothes had become worn out, and consequently she adopted male clothes. She gave the name of Mary Collett, but the authorities believe this is not her real name.

THE COUNCIL AND THE INQUIRY.

OFFICIALS AGGRIEVED AT THE NON-TRADERS' FINDINGS. --- Coun3el's Opinion to be Obtained.

The officials of the Corporation feel much aggrieved at the allegations of ignorance on their part contained in the Non-Trading Commissioners' report, and the matter was under consideration at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening. The DlAyoa informed the Council that he had received the reports of the Commissioners appointed to conduct the investigations. They were very. voluminous and required a great deal of what he would call "serious reading." He did not know that he had =Tithing to say about the matter particularly, but if the Council wished to discuss the reports in any shape or form, he thought it would be better to call a special meeting. (Hear, hear.) He had e,a very bad cold that evening,and it took himiall his time to speak. If the Council wished he would select Friday evening. (Cries of "No, no.") Councillor A. PEA.COCK su.ggested Thursday evening. Councillor WILKINSON did not think it was for the Council to discuss the matter at all. That was his opinion. Most people in Warrington would have the advantage of reading the reports, and the public should judge for themselves as to who was right and who was wrong. (Hear, hear.) The people were far better educated now than they were when he knem them 30 years ago, and would be able to fully and truthfully inquire into the matter. They hid hid discussion enough on the subject, and what good had it donl ?

The; 111.4.v0a said he had besn aiked by several mem- bers what was the best course to adopt in the matter The reports which hal ba3n presented ware very con- tradictory. MA.YOR : That being the case you are both right. More laughter.) Alderman PIERPOINT did not think that any good to the town could arise out of the discussion. The 111A.YOR That is what I contend.

Everybody had had an equal opportunity of making himself acquainted with what appeared in the reports, and he did not think that any, good could come to the town, and certAinly not to the' Council, by a discussion. The NI kYOR : The Commissioners have to the best of their ability acted as judges and given their decision. Are we going to form a Court of Appeal to go into their ideas? lam under the impression that the matter is best as it is. He did not consider the Non-Trading representatives were treating the Mayor and the Corporation with the respect due to them in sending the report all over the country. HAVE THE OFFICIALS BEEN LIBEIALED ?

On the proposition of Alderman SUTTON. the Council decided that a copy of the report of the Commissioners appointed by the Non-Trading Association, together with all papers and documents to which it relates, be laid before counsel and that such steps may be taken as he may advise. PARISH CHURCH RESTORATION.

Sale of Work to Clear off the Debt.

The congregation of the Warrington Parish Church is this week making what is hoped will be a final effort to extinguish the debt on the restoration fund. The numerous improvements recently carried out have entailed an expenditure of £2,500, and £250 is still required. It was with the object of raising that amount that a three days' sale of work was opened in the Fair- field schools on Thursday by the Hon. Ellinor and Elisabeth Wilson-Patten. The numerous stalls had been attractively arranged and were in charge of the following :—Parochial and china stall, Mesdames Willis, J. G. Craik, S. Taylor, P. Rylands, Dewhurst, William Fairclough, Farrell, Hall, Mawson, Hicks and Locker, Misses Queenie Taylor, Millington and Boyle; refresh- ment stall, Mrs. Stevenson, and the Misses Willis, Brooks, Price, Dewhurst, Willcocks, Neild, Woods, Brown and Evans; St. George's Mission stall, Mesdames Barrett, Knight, Perrin, Simpson and Miss Hetherington; St. Clement's Mission, Mesdames Cameron, Davies, Charlesworth. Sanders, Isherwood, Collin and Mr. Charlesworth ; G. F. S. stall, the Misses White, Dannett and Horton, Mrs. Collin and Mrs. Bottomley ; flower stall, Misses Rogers and Sankey; children's toy and sweet stall, Mrs. Lowe, Misses F. Lowe, M. Lowe, L. Bottomly, Dora Collin and Vera Collin.

The Rector presided at the opening ceremony and amongst those present were the Hon. Ellinor and Elisabeth Wilson-Patten, the Revs. M. Stevenson, E. C. E. Carleton, G. W. Dickinson, and A. S. Cameron, Mr. and Mrs. Robert Pierpoint, Mr. S. Taylor (people's warden). Alderman Bennett and Mr. C. White.

The RECTOR briefly alluded to the-- i;i;i'O'iy of the Parish Church, and pointed out that in all probability there had been a church on .the present site for 1,200 years. In expressing his obligation to the Hon. Misses Wilson-Patten for coming to open the sale, he said he felt it a 'privilege to have known the late Lord Winmarleigh. He was one of the four octogenarians of Warrington when he (the Rector) came to the town—Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. Beamont, Canon Hearne, and Lord Winmarleigh. They knew what he did for the town and for the 'Parish Church.

The Hon.ELLINoR WILSON-PATTEN' said ii-W7t-s-itiieat pleasure to be able to come among their old friends, and especially on such an occasion, for they. more than anyone perhaps, could feel love and regard for the old Church they used to Wend in their earliest childhood. They had spent a great deal of time that morning in looking over the alterations, and they agreed with the Rector that they were great improvements. They sin- cerely hoped the sale would be successful. (Applause.) Mr. R. PIERPOINT, M.P., moved a vote of thanks to the Hon. Misses Wilson-Patten:

Mr. S. TAYLOR seconded, and the'motion having been heartily carried, the sale commenced. On Thursday evening Mrs. Morley Stevenson and the members of the Training College Staff, together with Mr. W. E. Atkin presented the farce," A man of ideas" with which they created considerable amusement. The sales on they realized £BS. _ _ _

The sale yesterday should have been opened by Lady theenall who, however, was unable to attend owing to slight indisposition. The Rector, who again presided, announced that Lady Greenall had enclosed in her letter, expressing her regret at not being able to be present, a cheque for £25. (Applause.) The entertainments yesterday consisted of a concert arranged by Mr. F. Darby, and selections which were given by Miss Turner's mandoline band. The sale will be continued this afternoon and will be opened by Mrs.W.P.Rylandss A children's concert will be given in the afternoon, and in the evening a dramatic sketch will be given by the staff of the Training College.

Workingmen's Mission, Bank Quay.

Thl Christian Endeavour Society in connection with the above celebrated its 10th anniversary on Wednesday, when a tea and social gathering were held in the schoolroom, Thewlis-street, a large num- ber being present. After the tables had been cleared, a meeting was held, presided over by Mr. J. Hampson (president), who gave an address. Brief addresses were also given by Messrs. W. Worrall and W. Gray. During the course of the evening the members took the opportunity of presenting a beautiful rustic flower stand, to be kept in the chapel, in honour of the event, the members also undertaktng to keep the stand filled with flowers every Sunday. Mr. Hough (president of the mission) in a few suitable remarks accepted the present on behalf of the Chapel Committee, and also tendered their thanks to the society. Mrs. F. Gray and Miss Oakley contributed a duet, the first-named also singing a solo. Mr. F. Atkinson accompanied on the piano. The Secretary read her annual report,also giving a brief outline of the society's work during the past ten years. A vote of thanks to the caterers, on the motion of Mr. T. Dale, seconded by Mr. R. C. Tanner, and the singing of the Doxology, brought a very enjoyable meeting to a close.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

(By " Ovalist.")

The high and mighty Wiganers were reduced to a fit and proper humility on Saturday. They came to Warrington, as once before, fully intent on conquer- ing, but they met their masters and retired discomfited by nine points to two. There was little doubt in Warrington as to the probable issue of the match. If .the full strength of the Wirepullers was not equal to upsetting the pretensions of any visiting team, well it was about time they shut up shop, so to speak. Wigan were not feared in the least, and events proved that the colliers are not so formidable alside as has been reported. They possess plenty of energy, but not much science, and a smart back team with a respectable front rank ought to stand a good chance any time with the international conglomeration from Wigan.

The Warrington-Wigan fixture is always a popular one, and there was a splendid gathering ;at Wilders- pool of about 8,000 people, the gate-money realising the handsome total of £l7O. Play opened' very smartly, and both teams put in some good work in the first ten minutes. Warrington got through the visitors' defence in what appeared a perfectly legitimate way, but Dickenson's try was disallowed. Hockenhall should have made amends five minutes later, but he stumbled when he had a clear run in after smart passing. Wigan were thus very lucky to escape disaster, and it was only small consolation for Warrington when Prestoi placed a penalty goal—the first he has kicked since joining the " Wire- pullers." Wigan responded gamely and Leytham equalised matters with a good kick. From this point play deteriorated, neither side being able to get away from the close attentions of the other, and half-time arrived with the score unaltered.

In the second portion there was some very hard struggling, but thanks to very ragged scrummaging the ball never became free to either set of backs. Warrington were more than a match for the visitors at all points, but played with a lack of finish in direct contrast to the display of a week before. Time wore on and it really looked as if Wigan were going to divide the points. Warrington could neither kick a goal nor get a try, though they missed both by the narrowest of margins. Penalties were pretty frequent, and after Fish had failed, Preston took a long shot from near the touch-line, and recorded a splendid goal which brought down the house.

This point put Warrington on good terms with themselves, and they certainly deserved it. Wigan were somewhat disheartened and it was no surprise to see the home forwards a few minutes later Break away and carry all before them, Preston scoring a try, and afterwards adding a third goal. The Ex- Swintonian thus scored all the ni.ne points for War- rington—a unique feat particularly for a forward. This was the first time he had been given a fair chance to justify his reputation as a goal-kicker on Warrington's behalf, and he fairly established his position as the goalist of the team. The three gals he got were beauties, and he was less than a yard out with two other:shots. Preston reminds me in his methods, of Donaldson the famous forward who a few years ago kicked guts galore for Broughton Rangers. He understands the art well, and now he has found his place he should prove invaluable to the Warrington team.

A word should be said of the home forwards collec- tively. They met a lusty pack in the Wigan " scrum " but they had the better of the argument before the close—a contrast indeed from the case when last the teams met! With the Warrington backs not showily, their best form there was not much to choose between the respective sets. Wigan's famous "Pigeon," Harry Lowe was no match for Warrington's more famous Fish, and got little chance of fluttertng his veings. On the other side Harris and Leytham were well matched, though the Warrington man was obviously short of practice. The Warring- ton centres are not giving a very classy exhibition on the attack just now. There is a want of ingenuity which conies perhaps from the men playing so much together They rely on one another and are certainly getting into a groove. Dickenson by the way, should not try to take the ball one-handed. It is bad form, and loses chances. He was a great sinner in this respect on Saturday. Hockenhall was much ahead of D. Davies, and Hallam showed improved form. On the Wigan side Mason was very sound at full back, while Windsor Jones shaped well at centre. Anderson was as usual too fond of going on his own.

Leeds sustained a licking on Saturday at Halifax and now are only equal to Oldham, who did well to take full points from Wakefield. Salford fell further away by going down at home to the much-improved "Lions" of Swinton. The. Lancashire County team sustained a severe shock at Runcorn on Wednesday, when they were de- feated by Cheshire by a try to nil. Lancashire have got so accustomed to winning the County Champion- ship that I believe they imagine they have only to go on the field and chuck the ball about to win. On Wednesday they were thoroughly well beaten, and no team better deserved to win than did the Cheshire fifteen—or rather fourteen. Cook the Warrington for- ward, sustained a severe cut on the forehead which caused his retirement before half-time, but the Ches- hire men played like tigers afterwards and completely demoralized the Red Rose representatives. They were over weighted to begin with but they got the ball somehow,and the excellent understanding existing in the rear division enabled them to keep Lancashire well in hand. It was a big performance by Cheshire

and should go a long way towards securing them the championship.

I have not seen so keen a game this season. There was no parlour football, and the gruelling some men --particularly Lomas—got was enough to satisfy for a month. Probably a more ragged exhibition has never been given byla Lancashire Northern Union team. The combination was all at sea through the absence of men like Wilson, S. James, and Baxter. Anderson and W. James were like oil and water mixed, while Lomas and Taylor in the centre were equally disjointed. The Salford crack ruined the game by his selfishness. More than once a try would have accrued to Fish if Lomas had passed at the proper time instead of try- ing to plough his way through the toughest first of the defensive line. The only man who came out of the ordeal with honour, on the Lancashire side, was Poole, who played a great game after a somewhat un- steady commencement. The forwards put nothing like the energy into their work that the Cheshire men did, though Thomas and Ferguson were always to.the fore. There will be some radical change in the team to represent Yorkshire.

Warrington have a severe task to-day. Swintoti seem to have come into form, and their victory at Salford stamps them as a vastly improved side. Warrington rarely win on Swintori territory, but I think they should have an even chance to-day. The "Blues "usually play an open game, and Warrington are as good as the best at this style of football. Boardman and Cook are important absentees from the pack, but there should be nothina to fear in this direction. Swintonians have lately been sighing for Preston's goal-kicking. Let us hope they get more than enough of it to-day.

The Warrington team will be : Hallam.; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson, and Harris; Davies and Hockenhall ; Thomas, Shugars, Preston, Beltim, Jolley, Naylor, Morrison, and Forster.

The "A." team Nv ill be : Knight ; Short, Berry, Jordan, Brooks; J. Davies and Duckworth ; Harmer, Ashton, Coleman, Lunt, and another.

The case of J. Davies came before the Northern League Committee this week, when it was decided that sending off the field in the Oldham match was sufficient punishment.

Hockey. [BY R.G.P.] As foretold in my notes of last week, the Stockton Heath first eleven easily *defeated Bankfield, the score being 12-0; but the second string, playing with a some- what weak eleven, had to admit defeat by 44.

The game at home was so one-sided, that there were few points of real interest. Suffice to say the Stockton Heath forwards, although not playing with all that combination and understanding which is the science of the game managed to pile on goals. The halves were 'good, but as the Bankfield forwards were weak, they had not much chance of showing their skill, while the backs were seldom troubled.

The second eleven travelled to Bankileld with only ten men, and had to pay the penalty. Seeing what a lot of reserves Stockton Heath have this year, this was somewhat unfortunate. The game seems to have been very level, each side doing an equal amount of pressing, but the Stockton forwards were unable to shoot goals, and when Smith retired hurt, the home team had things all their own way.

Angling.

Last week-end marked the close of the season so far as local contests are concerned; though individual efforts, probably, will not be slackened by those who are of an ardent and enthusiastic nature. Now, the fish belonging to the various classes of what are generally termed, the" coarse" fish are at their best, and thus offering the best of sport when they are in a biting humour. Pike fishing is already in full swing and several good catches have recently been spoken of as having been obtained both from public and private waters. An interesting fact has been noted, in that, several gudgeon filled with spawn, have been taken from the Bridgewater Canal.

An open contest, protnoted for the benefit of J. Gorse, by the Perseverance Angling Society was decided in the waters of the Bridgewater Canal at Stockton Heath on Saturday afternoon last. There was a good entry of about 190 competitors and Messrs. William Bradbury',assisted by several work- ers, started the match in good time. The fishing was only of a moderate character, though some 94 contest- ants recorded weights. Eleven prizes of an 'aggregate value:of £5 3s. 6d. were offered. Result, Ist. H. Green, 11b. 30z. 6drs. ; 2nd. E. Gleave, Boz. 4drs : 3rd. S. Thomas, 7oz. Bdrs. ; 4th. T. Ashton ; sth. J. Brookes - 6th. J. Reams; 7th. J. Shepherd; Bth. R. Sister; 9th;. R. Cook; 10th. G. Hamblett ; and 11th. T. Wright. The prizes were distributed at the hesd-quarters of the club, the Cattle Market Ina on Monday evening.

An open competition was promoted at Lymm on Sat- urday afternoon but a very disappointing entry of corn- pecitors responded,only twenty seven drawing numbers. This is somewhat to be regretted considering that the prizes offered were well up to the average of other competitions. Messrs. F. J. mson, A. Bate and W. Kirkpatrick Bridgewater Arms, Lymm, had the manage- ment of the match. Sport was very poor indeed, the winner only scaling 4oz. 10drs. and the ten prizes offered were just won. Result :-Ist W. Goodwin, 2nd F. Poulton, 3rd James Hackney, 4th J. Bate, sth T. Clare, 6th W. Roberts, 7th E. Bowles, Bth IT, Sherratt, 9th A. Slirratt and 10th F. Smith. The prizes were distributed after the match.

Another open contest was fished at Vale Royal on Saturday last and the proceeds were devoted to the Winsford Hospital. Ninety-obe competitors put in an appearance and out of these fifty caught fish. There were 31 Prizes, and the heaviest weight recorded was over 11b. and the lowest taking a prize was sozs. A full list of winners has not come to hand.

There were a goodly number of local anglers engaged in fishing operations last week end. Sport was of a varying chvacter. A pike of Gibs. Boz. Gdrs. was captured at the rive; Gowy by Mr. T. Leigh. Mr. W. Hamblett had three nice dace, the largest probably 10oz. and Mr. Alf Maddock a roach of lloz. sdrs. The Bridgewater Canal yielded plenty of gudgeon, but there was nothing of any particular note registered from the old lived at Lyrnm. The river Weaver was not so productive even of small fish as it has been during previous week ends.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly beneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the pre- valent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adopted to the needs of the merchant and the workman, the professional man, and he who i wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the I student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a pre paration as contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym' Evans' Qninine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried, has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give relief, and we may say further, that it has proved per- manently beneficial, when other preparations at best gave only temporary relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache, Pains in the Side, Giddiness, Loss of Appetite, also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleeplessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits, and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness, either after an illness, long con finement to ill-ventilated rooms or any othe cause. it strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts of the system which have been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease. If you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by taking now a course of this excellent Tonic, which is sold everywhere in bottles 2s. 9d. and 4s. 6d. each, but should any difficulty be experienced in procuring it, the Proprietors will forward same for the above prices, carriage free. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ALLEGED UNLAWFUL WOUNDING. A sidelight on some of the lower life of Warrington was forthcoming at the police court on 'Monday when a pitiful story was unfolded to the magistrates. This was in the case in which Catherine Walsh, 8, Dial- Court, made her appearance in the dock on a charge of unlawfully wounding her husband:--The Chief Constable at the outset asked that the prisoner should be remanded as, he stated, the husband was still an inpatient in the Infirmary, and was not vet out of danger. On Saturday night the prisoner and her husband, who had been married for six years, were having, what the neigh- bours described as their usual week end quarrel, and were both drank, in fact everyone connected with the case was more or less under the influence of drink. During the quarrel the husband made unfounded accusa- tions against his wife and, the wife said, struck and kicked her. He was making to strike her again when she picked up a bottle and struck him • on the head. Probably she had no intention of inflicting such serious injuries as she did, as the man's skull was fractured and one of the main arteries in the head severed. He was taken in an unconscious condition to the Infirmary. The prisoner during her married life had led the life of a dog and had had a great deal to put up with.— Prisoner was remanded. FAILING TO NOTIFY SCARLET FEVER.

"'John Gill, 106, Padgate lane, was summoned at the Warrington Police Court on Monday, for failine to notify a case of scarlet fever on the 22nd of September. Mr. g. Wilson deputy town clerk,prosecuted and at the outset contended that in this case ignorance could not be pleaded as the defendant was an educated man and held a responsible position in the town. He explained that early in September the defendant's little son had an attack of scarlet fever though nothing was noticed until the Nth September when he started peeling. Two days later a second child commenced with the disease. In the second case the decease was attended with a rash Neither of the cases were reported to the medical officer of health and dnring the whole of the time another child was attending the Warrington Technical school The second case was subsequently reported by Dr. Ferguson who was called in to attend to the child, though he was not called in until a fortnight after it commenced with the disease.—Evidence in support of Mr. Wilson's statement was given by Inspector Flood. —Mr. Steel, who appeared for the defendant, stated that the second child mentioned was a delicate child, and during September broke out with sores round the mouth. The mother accordingly kept her at home. The school attendance officer shortly afterwards visited the house and Mrs. Gill being informed that she must obtain a medical certificate in order to keep the child at home, called in Dr. Ferguson. Prior to Dr. Ferguson's visit she had not the slightest idea that the child was suffering from scarlet fever as the disease was not attended by any of the symptoms with which it was generally accompanied.—Evidence in support of the statement was given by Mr. and Mrs. Gill, and by Dr. Ferguson who said he did not think the defendant or his wife had the slightest suspicion that the child was suffering from scarlet fever when be was called in.—The magistrates considered the case was one in which the defendant had been guilty of carelessness, and fined him n the costs.

Warrington Borough Police Court.

Minor Cases.

MONDAY.—Before the Mayor (presiding). Alderman Smethurst, Councillors Thornton and Wilkinson, and Messrs. T. Birchall, William Roberts and S.

Wright. AFTER Fotirt YEARS.—Islac Lomax, 8, Blackhurst- street, was charged with assaulting Patrick and Mary Fallen, neighbours, in June 1900. Patrick Fallen said that prisoner'knocked him on the floor and jumped on his chest with his knees. He also bit his flnger.—The prosecutrix stated that prisoner caught hold of her hair and knocked her down. There were no previous con- victions against the prisoner, and he was fined 10s. and costs, or fourteen days' imprisonment in default. LILLIPUTIAN WRECKERS.—Five boys whose ages ranged from six to ten years, were charged with doing damage to the shrubs in the Park on October 16th. Their names were:—Walter Philpot,2,Twiss-yard, Cockhedge ; James Smith, 3, Garden-row; Caleb Wernora, 2, Garden- row, Thomas Green, 11, Garden-row; and William Taylor 36, Cockhedge-lane. Detective Clough said that he was on duty in Sankey-steet when he saw the boys carrying sticks. When they saw him they ran away, but he managed to catch one of them. He proceeded to the Park, and found that two elder bushes had been com- pletely broken. Fines of 3s. 6d. were inflicted in each case.—ln a second case two boys, William Waywell, 88, Folly-lane, and William Lythgoe, 61, Longshaw-street, aged 10 years, were summoned for throwing stones in Dallam-lane on October 14.—James Morris, employed at Wyman's Foundry, said a window had been broken by the boys throwing stones. Several windows had been broken on other occasions and stone-throwing had be- come a great danger to the men employed there. Ihe boys were fined 3s. 6d. each. DISORDERLY SCENE IN THE CEMETERY.—Herbert Molyneux, 58, Ellesmere-street; George Monks, 44 Lord Nelson-street; and William ' Bridge 26, Liverpool, road, were summoned for committing a breach of the peace by disorderly behaviour in the Cemetery on October 16th.—Detective Clough said he had received special instructions to visit the Cemetery, and saw the defendants sitting on a seat there. They commenced jostling one another about and were also shouting. He watched them for about three-quarters of an hour. They also called after young ladies as they passed, and caused considerable commotion.—The Chief Constable said the

Cemetery would be a by no means healthy place for young men to carry on in this fashion in the future. Special men would see that order .was kept and any offenders would be promptly brought up and the case pressed. He was sorry to see respectable young men like the defendants in that position, and he hoped the publicity given to the cases would serve as a warning to others.—This being their first offence the defendants were ordered to pay 3s. 6d.

ADULTERATED JAM.

Warrington Grocer Fined.

An interesting case was heard at the Warrington Police Court yesterday, when a local grocer, was sum- moned for selling two pounds of plum jam not of the quality and substance required by the purchaser, in that it contained a quantity of salicylic acid. Defendant pleaded guilty. Mr. S. Wilson, deputy town clerk, prosecuted and at the outset read a letter banded to him by the defendant and addressed to the defendant by a Mr. Thomas Reed, Peasley Cross, St. Helens. In the letter Mr. Reed said "I have written to the makers of the jam who have written to say that the jam is made the same way as all other jams are made. The makers say the acid is a preservative and that used in small quantities, it is not harmful. I am innocent in the matter as I did not know the acid was put in the jam, but if you should be fined I will refund the amount of the fine as I am better able to bear the loss." Continuing, Mr. Wilson stated that on the 21st of last month Inspector Flood visited the defendant's shop and purchased a two-pound jar of jam, which, as was the custom, he divided into three parts, one of which he sent to the public analyst, who certified that the jam contained 3 grains of salicylic acid per pound. The acid had a serious effect upon the human system as it was a deleterious and dangerous drug, affecting the me-abranes of the stomach, and also the nervous system. Upon a strong healthy individual the amount contained in the jam would have no effect, but in the case of a delicate adult or child it would result in very serious consequences if the jam was eaten continually. He would point out that the acid was used as a preservative whereas if the jam was properly made it was not needed. One important factor in the case was that the defdndant was not the real culprit, but as he had before stated the defendant would protect himself by getting a certificate as to the purity of the jam from the makers. He sub- mitted that that was a proper case for the inflicting of a heavy penalty. Inspector Flood stated that the jam was described as "Cheshire farm house jam made from the finest fruits and cane sugar." He had been unable to ascertain the name of the makers. _ _ _ .

Dr. Gornall gave evidence as to the effect of the salicylic acid upon the human system, bearing out the statement made by Mr. Wilson. Mr. Ruddock, public analyst, gave evidence as to the amount nf the acid contained in the jam. Defendant stated he sold The jam in exactly the same condition as he bought it. He did net know it con- tained any foreign substance. The Mayor said the magistrates did not regard the case as a serious one and would fine defendant in the costs, together with the analyst's fee £1 9s. in all. Personally had he been sitting on a jury he would have required more evidence as to the culpability of the iefendant before he would have convicted.

DISFIGURED BY ECZEMA. Zam-Buk Cures a Life-Long Case.

" Eczema broke out on my face, neck and arms, and my .head was literally covered with sores." Thus Miss Elizabeth Ann Wood informed a "Bolton Chronicle" reporter when describing her marvellous cure through Zam-Buk • after life-long agony and dis- figurement. Miss Wood is a young mill-card tenter, and lives with her father, Mr. James Wood, of 3, Hope- lield-street, Bolton. "The disease commenced in my youngest days." said Miss Wood. "It began directly after, vaccination. My parents had an awful time with me. The sores ' mattered ' and spread until I was ompletely dis- figured," We were terribly upset," interposed her father. "We called in several doctors, but in vain, for the blotches remained and my child in her agony would scratch, scratch, scratch. We were told that the disease would take a tura when she was seven years old; but no change came. Then we were told we would have to wait until she was fourteen. Even then she was almost as bad as ever, and we began to look upon the case as hopeless. In all, we tried at least nine doctors, and wasted a small fortune."

_ "As I grew up," resumed Miss Wood," I suffered a martyrdom. On my bad days I was positively ashamed of my appearance, and very frequently I had to stay from my work. Blotched on the face and neck, and my hair coming off in handfuls, I could not mix even with my friends. Doctors were useless in my case, and we had to look out for some other source of relief. Noticing an account of Zam-Buk in a local paper, we decided to give it a trial. Everywhere my skin is as healthy as it ought to be. lam happier than ever I have been. I I can attend my work regularly, and I can go about as an ordinary mortal, instead of feeling I was set apart from the rest of womankind by a most disagreeableand troublesome disease. I shall always sing the praises of Zarn-Bnk, which is a balm of rare merit." Mr. Wood and four others confirmed his daughter's story ; and throughout the district Zam-Buk's extra- ordinary success has caused no little sensation. ROBBERIES AT WARRINGTON

Of late there has been a series of robberies and burglaries at Warrington, and a few weeks ago visits were paid by burglars to several well-known hotels in the town, considergile sums in cash, being stolen. Yesterday week a Mr. J. Forster cycled down Man- chester-road, and called at the Cemetery Hotel, leaving his machine at the doorway. He was absent for a few minutes only, but on leaving the. hotel he found his machine had gone. Suspecting a practical joke he made enquiries which revealed to him the fact that his machine had been stolen. No trace of it has, as yet, been forthcoming. On Tuesday,ithe residents in Sankey, became aware of the fact that robbers had visited that district, for dur- ing the previous night the lock-up shop of Mr. Ireland cycle maker, was broken into. Upon. examining his shop Mr. Ireland found 'that an entrance had been effected by the bursting open of .the door. Two bicycles, a plating bath and apparatus, and a number of cycle accessories in all to the value of about £3O had been stolen. • A lady's glove was found in the shop, the ownership of which Mr. Ireland has been unable to establish.

On Tuesday a rough-looking customer, who is believed to be John Foster, and who declines to give the police any information about himself, attempted to rob the till in the shop of Mrs. West, 31, Walton- road, Stockton Heath. About 9 a.m., Mrs. West, who was in the kitchen noticed a shadow on the wall of the shop. She went to investigate, and found, she states, the man Foster with his hand in the till which contained £ll 6s. in cash. Foster, on seeing Mrs. West immediately decamped, only to be arrested later by P.C. Williams. On Wednesday he was brought before Mr. Neild at the Warrington Occasional Court, and remanded to the Daresbury Petty Sessions. Foster when arrested said" It is a mistake. This is the first I have heard of it. It must be someone else."

He did not say he would go as far as Councillor !Stott, land debar a man for ever from trading with the Corporation but he certainly would put on some penalty or remove the standing order altogether It was a farce if they did notsee itproperly carried out. The MAYOR observed that during the year they had had something like ten discussions on things of that sort, and they had almost all come at the end of the meetings.
