ANGRY COUNCIL SCEL

COUNCILLOR MAGINN THREATENS PERSONAL VIOLENCE.

The Fair Wage Question.

CouncillorS I'r 'made a third attempt at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening to secure the adoption of a penalty clause to be enforced in the event of breaches of the Corporation contracts fair wages clauses recently adipted by the Council. The discus- sion which ensued upon his proposal witnessed an angry scene between himself and Councillor Maginn, the latter gentleman going so far as to threaten to pull Councillor Stott's head off. Councillor STorr moved "That Is Council adopt the following standing order: 'Any contractor having been proved by the Council to have broken the con- ditions of a contract, as a penalty for the first offence the Council shall not c msider any tender submitted by him for a period of one year from the date of the contract having been broken; for a second offence the penalty shall be two years, and for a third offence no tender for any work shall in future be considered by this Council from that contractor.'" In speaking to the motion Councillor Stott said it was an almost similar motion to that which he had previously introduced, except that they had brought the penalty down to what they considered was a minimum, and they asked that it should be on a sliding scale basis. He had been told many times that he had no right to bring such resolutions, but on this point he contended that the trade unionists were deserving of consideration and had some right to receive respect from an elected body like the Council. He did not bring these motions forward with the intention of making an attack upon any employer, or any set of employers, but in the interests of those employers who treated their men fairly and in order to protect those employers against men who lived to cut down the wages of their workpeople. It had been sugges- ted that a money penalty should be inflicted, but he would point out that in Bradford it had been found impossible to enforce a money penalty. He thought his proposal was a very reasonable one, and that the least the Council could do was to give it a fair trial. (Hear, hear.) Councillor BARTON seconded. Councillor PEIEBERTON asked what would be the effect if the resolution was carried and the Council found that a contractor, who had held a contract for some time—a builder working through a sub-cm- tractor—was doing work for the Corporation with men who were not paid the standard rate of wages. The TOWN CLERK: The contract would be taken from him. _ _ .

--- --- Councillor PEMBERTON : In that event would the Corporation have to finish the work? The TOWN CLERK said he did not know what would be their position in such an event. Councillor PEMBERTON : It might result in a loss of thousands of pounds. Continuing he said if the resolu- tion had said that no more contracts should go to a tenderer who offended,or that a contractor should not be allowed to proceed with a contract until he paid the standing rate, he would be prepared to support it. The MAYOR said it seemed to him that if a contractor committed a mere technical offence, which might be the result even of a clerical error, he would be punished as severely as the man who wilfully set them at defiance. If they adopted that resolution the only way would be that every one tendering for Corporation work should get a certificate from the Independent Labour Party that they were fit and proper persons to tender. If they were going to adopt this resolution they would be making someone judges who did not probably understand the ease. (Hear, hear). The punishment was so drastic that it would bring the standing orders into contempt. (Hear, hear) They might, however, fix the punishment so that it could be adjusted to the offence, trivial or otherwise, by fixing the period as not exceeding a cer- tain time, but if a man broke the standing orders they did not need a clause of this description. Councillor MAOLNN said no man had greater respect for trade unionism than he had, but he had no more respect for a combined body of men belonging to a trade union than he had for a number of honest men who did not belong to a union. (Applause). What would they say if because a man came from a certain country, belonged to a certain political party. or because he did not belong to a certain party, or because he did or did not profess a certain religion, or did not combine with a certain stronger power in a dis- trict, he was put outside the pale of society and not allowed to earn his living? He nad just as much respect for trade unionism if it was conducted for the benefit of workingmen as ever Councillor Stott had, but he wished to point out to Councillor Stott that if they had to conform to the conditions which extremists like Councillor Stott would like them to adopt,it would be simply impossible for trade unionists— Councillor STOTT: I rise to a point of order. It is not a question of employing trade unionists but of the trade union rate of wages. The Maxoa: We see it from a different point of view. Councillor MAOISM said that if Councillor Stott's resolution were passed it would mean that it would be impossible, absolutely impossible, for a clever working man or a working man of ambition to get past that mediocre position in which he might be placed. Un- fortunately trade unions had during the last few years worked unwisely with the result that a workingman would never be able to rise to the position of an em- ployer. The position of the employer would under these circumstances become hereditary and workmen would remain workmen all their lives. He would always like to see the energetic, industrious and persevering man with a chance of rising. These extremists wanted sato be reapers and not sowers. (Hear, hear.) They all came in at the harvest but never didanything to put a plough to the ground or sow. As to this very innocent look- ing motion, he had no objection to it as a motion or to the punishment as a punishment, but he did object to the ridiculous standing order which had already been passed on Councillor Stott's motion, and particularly No. 3, which stated that no contractor should sub-con- tract without the consent of the Council. Was there a single individual manufacturer whom they could ask in this way to give away the secrets of his trade? Councillor STOTT : We are not discussing what was dene two months ago. The MA.YOR : The one arises out of the other in this instance. Councillor MA GNNN I know you have threatened me in St. John's Ward, and, , of course, your object now is to prevent me explaining the Standing Orders. Continuing, he reiterated his question as to whether there was a single manufacturer who manufactured a part only of the goods he supplied who was likely to go to the Council and give away the secrets of his trade by saying that he bought this or that from such and such a man. Was it likely that if he sold furniture he would tell the buyer of whom he bought it? It was a ridiculous standing order. It was a standing disgrace to commonsense. The "reapers" were the only people who would frame such a standing order, and then come on with this proposed penalty. It more than met the crime ; it would kill the criminal. (Laughter.) He objected to the motion because it was a support to the ridiculous "No. 3" standing order. He would oppose it for all he was worth. He wished the public to know that at the next monthly meeting he would do his best to rescind the ridiculous stand- ing order and that whether he was returned again for St. John's or not. Alderman SMETHURST said the resolution was one of the most intolerable and un English resolutions ever put on paper.(Hear,hear ).Here they had a resolution brought in by a gentleman like Mr. Stott, to penalize the trades- people of that town. After the last meeting a great many people—tradespeople of that town—had protested to him against it, and he had there a letter from a big firm thanking him for his services. They pointed out that they had men in their employ earning ,C 2 ss. and ' £2 10s. who if they were of the stamp Councillor Stott would like, would have to accept their 20s. or 255. a week. It was a ridiculous thing for any gentleman to go and listen to such resolutions, let alone pass them. (Hear, hear). He hoped the Council would do as before, and decline to pass it. (Hear,hear). Councillor WILKINSON was sorry the Council had rambled so from the point. Many years ago they passed a standing order of the Council, which ruled and

INFECTIOUS DISEASES.

Professor W. R Smith Lectures on Their Cause and Prevention.

CONSUMPTION NOT HEREDITARY.

At the Parr Hall on Wednesday evening the first of a course of popular health lectures arranged by the Health Committee of the frown Council in conjunction with the Royal Institute of Public Health, wasdelivered by Professor Wm. R. Smith, M.D., D. Sc., F.R.S. (Edinburgh), President of the Royal Institute of Public Health, whose subject was "Infectious diseases: their causes and their prevention." Although the exception- ally modest charge of one penny was made for admis- sion, ,there was but a moderate attendance. Alderman Sutton, chairman of the Health Committee, presided. Professor SMITH said the object of the lectures was to bring home to the listeners a few points in connec- i tion with the important subject of the public health 1 which would, it was hoped, not only give them a more I intelligent concern in those matters which tended to the preservation of their own health, but would also lead them to appreciate some of the reasons which guided the Council as the public health authority of their district in the action which from time to time they took under the powers vested in them by the various Acts of Parliament. Sanitary science, about which so much had been said during the past few years, had for its aim the prevention of disease, the preserva- tion of health, and the prolongation of life—in other words, the maintenance of our people in a state of the highest efficiency for the labours of peace or the struggles of war. That night he ,would confine his remarks to that branch of the science which concerned nations or communities. The subject was • large and comprehensive, for it dealt with persons of every rank of both sexes and of every age. It took cognisance of the places and houses in which they lived of their occupations and modes of life, of thel food they ate and the water they drank. The professor em- phasised the value of studying the past history of • disease, for it taught them that several fatal epidemics, , some imported and some bred at home, had ceased to I trouble us because we had given up the habits which I welcomed or produced them, while some endemic maladies had disappeared with a wide and better culti- vation of the soil and others with the spread of commerce and the increase in the number I and variety of our articles of food. By I vaccination much suffering had been averted and many lives saved. Cure or alleviation was the aim ;of the physician prevention that of the sanatarian. I The oneconsidered the unit, the other the welfare, of the mass. Health, it was but a mere commonplace to say, was the greatest of all blessings—the mainspring of bodily strength and mental efficiency and the ! principal source of comfort and enjoyment. Disease, ;in proportion to its degree and duration, was rsynonymous with suffering more or less severe and failure more or less complete. It had also a relation to the State. Labour and capital, might be said to be the two pillars on which the iState rested. Disease paralysed labour I and wasted capital. Professor Smith briefly sketched the life of Pasteur who he said, raised the veil which for centuries had covered infectious diseases for he dis- covered their microbean origin.

===== 0002601_19041029_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSUMPTION.

No disease had of late years perhaps created so much attention both on the part of the State and the public as tuberculosis. Three Royal commissions within our time had been appointed to report on the question and there was a general disposition to provide suitable sanatoria for the treattrient of the disease. Death from tuberculous disease in all its forms had steadily fallen from 3,483 per million during the decade 1851 to 1860 to 2,000 during the decade 1890 to 1899, a diminu- tion of 409 per cent. There had been a further decrease to 1,899 in 1900, making a total reduction of 454 per cent. Also for every age period for which statistics had been obtained there had been a decrease, sometimes of a marked character. The world was deeply indebted to that eminent scientist, Dr. Koch,for his most brilliant discovery which enabled us to know the exact cause of the disease and to devise means to retard its progress and cause its destruction. Before the discovery, consumption was always consid- ered to be an hereditary diSease and the greatest pains were taken to trace the disease to some worthy or un- worthy ancestor whenever it attacked, as it often did, our fairest and best beloved. But all that was now a thing of the past. The disease was only transmitted through the agency of the bacillus, and that being the case heredity as we ordinarily used the term could account for very few, in fact none of the oases of tuberculosis that occurred either in man or animals. Children did not inherit tuberculosis from their parents although undoubtedly they might inherit such a weak- ness of constitution and of the different parts of the body as would enable them to harbour and nourish the parasites which had caused such trouble to the parents. All that was necessary to enable the child to withstand the attacks of the bacillus was to strengthen the tissues and build up the constitution with good food, fresh air, sunlight, and plenty of exercise, and try to place the child in such conditions that the bacillus could not obtain access to it. There would then come a time when a resistance on the part of the child would be built up, as effective as that possessed by a healthy child born of healthy parents. While some persons were more prone to it as to other diseases by having debilitated constitutions, living unhealthy lives or living amidst insanitary surroundings, every case of consumption must arise from some pre-exist- ing case. The lecturer showed bow a great reduction in the number of deaths from consumption was due to improved sanitary measures, and a higher standard of comfort and cleanliness. The disease was contagious, being communicated from person to person and from animal to man, and it did not arise in any way. If the disease had been in any way com- parable to the infectious diseases with which we were more actively concerned, steps would have been taken long ago by the sanitary authorities to banish the sources of infection. The two chief causes of infection were the sputum coughed up by people suffering from consumption, and the milk given by cows sufferirg from tuberculous udders. The Professor showed how uncleanliness, bad ventilation, and intemperance were all causes of tuberculosis, and particularly urged his hearers to note the desirability of fresh air by night and day for themselves and their children. "Do not be afraid "he said "to open your windows and keep them open. Allow no lodgment of dust or dirt in your dwellings. Remember that moist cleansing is better than mere sweeping."

SMALLPDX. Dealing with this disease, Professor Smith said he was given to understand that they were a very enlightened population in Warrington. There might be one or two so-called conscientious objectors but they were very few and far between. (Applause.) The sub- ject of smallpox and vaccination during the past two years had naturally engrossed much attention particu- larly in London, where an epidemic had occurred, and the subject of vaccination must shortly come again before Parliament. The lecturer alluded to the dis- covery of the efficacy of vaccination by Edward Jenner and pointed out that the injection of the lymph pre- vented smallpox in a great number of cases and when it did not prevent attacks it mitigated their severity. He quoted striking figures to bear out his statement and emphasised the fact that in spite of the loathsome nature of smallpox in its severer forms the disease was not serious when aperson was vaccinated. The Professor then touched upon diphtheria and the value of treat- ment by the anti-toxic serum. MEASLES.

This disease, he understood, was unfortunately too prevalent in Warrington. lie was given to understand that the popular idea about the disease held 'good here. A great many mothers thought it was necessary for a child to have measles and the sooner it had the disease the better. If a child was not going to get the measles easily and it happened that another child had measles

in the house he had known of parents putting the healthy child in the same bed with the measly child in order that the disease might be con- tracted. He would impress upon his hearers that all the other infectious diseases he had referred to put together did not produce a mortality as great as that produced by measles which was the most fatal of all infectious diseases. There was no necessity in the world for a child to have measles, no more than there was a necessity for it to have pthisis, scarlet fever or diphtheria. Unfortunately it was extremely infectious, perhaps the most infectious of all infectious diseases. Children who were subject to measles ought to be taken the greatest possible care of ; they should be kept in and prevented from catching cold. The lecturer asked his hearers to bear in mind that there was a duty to one's neighbour in the matter of isolation. He also remarked that the local educatioa authority were to be congratulated upon having realised the importance of medical science in connection:with their schools and in loppointing such an eminent man as the Medical Officer of Health for Warrington to be their medical officer. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19041029_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
SCARLET FEVER.

They did not, said the LECTURER, know the cause of this disease and it was therefore extremely difficult to deal with. It was a serious disease, and the Warring- ton health authority as represented by the Town Council had erected hospitals for the treatment of it. He had heard with very great satisfaction that a very large percentage, something like 92 or 94, of the total cases of scarlet fever in this town obtained access to the hospital. There was perhaps a doubt in the minds of some as to the wisdom of the provision of infectious hospitals, but he had no doubt %whatever that such an institution was.a great boon to a community such as this. The provision in Warrington was very admirable and deserved every possible encouragement. In conclusion the Professor asked his hearers to take a deeper interest in the subject of preventive medicine and to give every possible encouragement to their representatives on the Town Council to carry out in an energetic manner all preventive measures for the preservation of the health of the inhabitants. The care of the public health was the duty of all interested in the progress and welfare of the nation. (Applause.) VOTE OF THANKS.

_ Alderman SUTTON moved a move of thanks to Pro- fessor Smith for his lecture. He said the Health Com- mittee were anxious that as many inhabitants of War- rington as possible should attend the course of lectures in order that they might gain information which would be of great value to them in life. (Hear, hear). He expressed regret that, with such an eminent lecturer, the hall was not crowded that night, as every encourage- ment had been afforded to the people to come. Mr. C. WHITE '3econded. He assured the audience that n.s the oldest practitioner in the town he considered the lecture the most valuable and instructive that could have been delivered on such an important subject. Pre- vention was ten times better than cure, and if everyone took to heart what the Professor had said about good air,fgood water, cheerful and well-lighted homes, and plenty of exercise, the doctors would suffer very much. (Laughter.) If the people took the lessons thoroughly to heart there would be a differont state of things in Warrington. There was plenty for the Health Com- mittee to do, although what had been already done was exceedingly creditable to them. _

The motion was heartily agreed to, and in returning thanks, Professor SMITH paid a tribute to Alderman Sutton's interest in all thatconcerned the public health.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

TILE WIDNES ASSAULT CASE.

Peter Hill, 57 Tunnel-road, Liverpool, was again remanded at Widnes, on Thursday, for a week on the char,re of maliciously wounding Alfred Ernest Johnson, Widnes-road, on the 19th inst. Superintendent Strick- land said that his condition was qnly slightly improv- ing, and he would have to ask for a further remand for a week. The bench granted this, and bail of one £5O and two sums of 220. WIDNES GIRL JUMPS OFF ABRIDGE.

_ At Widnes, on Thursday, Annie Jackson, Denton- street, Widnes, was ordered to go to a Wesleyan Home. On Wednesday last she attempted to commit suicide by jumping off the railway bridge at Appleton on to the Cheshire Lines Railway—a distance of eighteen feet. A goods train was approaching at the time, but her perilous position was noticed by two platelayers, who carried her into a place of safety, and thence to her home. She told her rescuers that at home they were always "pick- ing" at her, and she wished she was dead. She wept bitterly in court, and promised not to repeat the attempt again and to go to a home provided for her, nd stay there until a situation was found for her.

IMPOSING ON AN EARLESTOWN DOCTOR. On Thursday at Newton-le-Willows, James Heskin, aged twenty-two, a Liverpool youth, who has been an inmate of the Liverpool Reformatory School, Newton- le-Willows, was charge(' with attempting to obtain £1 by false pretences from Dr. Harold E. Gd, and in each case the usual order was made. BURTONWOOD FARMERS FINED AT NEWTON.

On Thursday, at Newton-le-Willows, Edwin Fair- dough, farmer, of Burtonwood, was fined 10s. and costs for cruelty to a horse, which was said to have had ca,nkered feet so badly that the feet were rotten and the bone exposed. The defence was that defendant followed the instructions of the inspector, tried to cure the disease, and was guilty of an error of judgment in turning the animal out to grass.—William Haslam, also a farmer at Burtonwood, was charged with cruelty to a horse by allowing it to be in the roadway with a lame leg. It transpired that defendant had sold the animal "seen or unseeß," and the man who purchased it on learning that the animal was lame preferred to lose his money than take possession. Defendant was fined 10s. and costs.

BILLIARDS. THE STROKES OF THE GAME.

IEL lii3 C) Xi 71hTI.

[COPYRIGHT.]

SlDE.—continued.

It will have been noticed that in the stroke 13hown on Diagram 19 (last week), the side trans Initted to the object ball is the reverse of the 6ide given to the cue ball, and this must always be the case with transmitted side. Side is given to a cue ball by striking it on its edge in the direction it afterwards travels, whereas an ob- ,ject ball can never be hit on its edge in the direc- tion it afterwards travels. For no matter whether an object 'ball be hit very full or quite finely, it must always travel in the direction Which is a continuation of a line passing through the centres of the balls at the moment ,Of contact, so that an object ball is really always nit exactly on the centre as regards its ultimate Dath. The side that is transmitted to an object hall must therefore be imparted to it in quite a ,different manner from the way side is imparted le a cue ball. When a cue ball rapidly rotating with side Strikes another ball, the tendency is to cause the the ball to rotate the reverse way, much in the same way that two cog wheels revolve in reverse directions. As, however, the duration of time that the balls are in contact with each Other (when away from any cushion) is such a Minute fraction of a second, the cue ball has no time to give more than a rotatory rub to the °biect ball, and this is the reason why so little side is transmitted to an object ball.

DIAGRAM 20.

TEE sew-back cannon.—lf the cue ball be struck With plenty of side a slight amount is trans- tatted to the object ball.

In many cases the side transmitted to an ob- iect ball appears to be more than it really is. The stroke shown on Diagram 20 illustrates a ease in point. Let it be assumed that a screw back cannon without side is played, and that the red is struck in such a manner as to cause it to travel down the table and back again as shown 133, the thick intersected line. If instead of having played the stroke without any side, strong right hand side had been imparted to the cue ball, the red ball would have had a little side transmitted to it, and this, as already explained, 'Would be the reverse of the side given to the cue ball. The red ball would therefore have struck the baulk cushion with just slight left-hand side. and would have come off at a slightly different angle, more or less, as shown by the thin inter- eeeted line on the diagram, and 'though the ,difference between the two angles must always 'be very, little indeed, yet, owing to the distance that the object ball has to travel before it leaches the top cushion, the two different lines of the ball's return may impinge on the cushion a considerable distance from each other. . * * • • * • * *

A fact that is quite unknown to the ordinary Player, and even to many good players, is that a cushion may impart side to a cue ball or an Object, ball. On tables with very fast cushions Made of soft yielding rubber, the amount of side which under certain circumstances may be com- Municated to a ball by a cushion is often very uoticeable indee4 ; especially is this so if the cushions have a very sharp nose. On tables with such cushions the stroke shown on Diagram 22 can be played.

DIAGRAM 21,

The Ordinary angle of reflection of a nlai stroke; ,slow to medium pace.

" An exam nigh-speed resilient c'

_ .ch very cushioni

If, however, the cushions are very yielding, and the cue-ball (aimed at the same point on the oppo- site cushion and without any side) be struck with great force, the cushion will throw it off nearly straight across the table, and the rebound from the second cushion will often be at a considerable angle towards baulk as shown on Diagram 22; so that when it has finished rolling the ball may, easily be considerably nearer the baulk line than the part of the cushion which was first struck. Diagram 22 shows this eccentric angling of a plain fast ball, which occurs more or less on all tables with very resilient cushions. The reason that such a stroke occurs with very yielding cushions is as follows :—When a ball strikes a very resilient cushion with great •force the ball (provided the angle of incidence is not very acute) is pressed so much into the rubber that the ordinary angle of reflection is quite destroyed, and the cushion throws the ball off at an angle more or less approaching a right angle. When, however, a cushion throws a ball off in this :planner it also imparts a con- siderable amount •of side to it (the ball apparently gets a twist owing to its being buried in the cushion and thrown off at an angle totally different from the ordinary angle of re- flection) and this side is often very plainly notice- able when the ball strikes the second cushion. Diagram 22 shows the effect of this cushion- imparted side, which, of course, takes effect when the second cushion is struck. (To be Continued.)

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WIGAN " A " v. !WARRINGTON "A."

These teams with an unbeaten record met at Wigan before 6,000 spectators. Wigan pressed from a couple of free kicks, but Jones spoiled a passing movement by sticking to the leather. Duckworth and Davies removed hostilities by a combined effort, and a mull between Green and Sharrock looked dangerous. The Wigan forwards went away with a rush, and reached the visitors' "25." Wigan were awarded a free kick, and Sharrock's attempt at goal was only a few inches wide. Warrington'attacked after the drop out. A brilliant piece of passing, in which all the visitors' backs handled the leather, ended in Brookes scoring near the corner flag. Knight added a goal. The Warrington men were warmly cheered for this, and a subsequent effort gained considerable ground. The Wigan backs mulled re- peatedly, though on one occasion Carey made a fine breakaway. Warrington, however, continued to have the best of the game, and Jones was tackled under the Winn posts. Half-time :—Warrington " A," 1 goal 1 try (5 points); Wigan " A." nothing. The visitors again attacked on resuming,and Sharrocks bad a kick charged down. Carey was to the fore with another breakaway, but nothing resulted. The Wigan backs played very poorly, Jones giving a weak dis- play at half. The game was stopped for a few minutes pwing to an injury to Knight, who afterwards resumed. Hector scored a grand try for Wigan. The same player failed at goal. Warribgton had the better of matters after this and Forster scored two tries, the final result being : Warrington, 1 goal 3 tries (11 points) ; Wigan 1 goal 1. try (5 points). , OLDHAM (A) v. ROCHDALE HORNETS (A).

At Oldham. The chief interest in this match from the Oldham point of view lay in the appearance of the new centre threequarter whose services Oldham have secured. His name is Thomas McLean, and he played last season with the Cardiff Reserve, and for the early part of this season he operated at centre in the Cardiff first team. He gave an exhibition of his ability to a delighted crowd at Watersheddings, and gave abundant evidence of being a promising recruit. He scored two tries and kicked the only goal of the match, and repeatedly showed that he is a sprinter. The game was a very one-sided affair. The scorers for Oldham were Oardner, Williams, McLean and Lewis. Final : Oldham (A), 1 goal 7 tries (23 points) ; Rochdale Hornets (A), nil.

Cairo-street Literary and Debating Society. At a meeting of the above society held on Tuesday evening, an interesting and highly instructive dis- cussion took place on the temperance question. On being called upon by the chairman to move "That this society is in favour of total abstinence," Mr. Fred Sherburn very ably opened the debate by speaking of the baneful efk'ct of alcohol on the physical and the mental system. He also drew attention to the vast numbers of people who, through habits of intemper- ance, found their way into the workhouses,prisons,and lunatic-asylums. Further, he pointed out that a great deal of the bribery which took place at elections was ' due to the influence of drink, and held, that, in view of , the many evils resulting from intemperance, abstinence from alcoholic liquors for the sake of ex-, ample, was worthy of all right-minded people. Laters Mr. John Monks was called upon to move "That this society approves of temperance."and,in the course of a well-conceived address, explained his attitude towards the temperance cause. Although he had no reason whatever to disapprove of total abstinence on the part of those who were willing to follow it out, he did not think that any serious objection could be raised to a moderate use of alcohol. Speaking• of pledges, he said that, personally, he did not approve of their use, inasmuch as they restricted the freedom of the will, and were in most cases taken during early youth, when the individual was ,incapable of realizing the responsibility he or she was undertaking. He also made a reference .to the power of acquired character over heredity, Pad maintained that a temperate use of alcoholic lig, ors often needed the exercise of will- power, and thus helped to develop it. Several others took part in the discussion, and, when the motions were put to the vote sixteen members sided with Mr. Sherhurn and five with Mr. Monks.

===== 0002601_19041029_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR NOTES.

Very general dissatisfaction is expressed at the fact that steam cars were not allowed to compete against petrol vehicles at the recent Blackpool races. .

In one case, the entrance fee had actually been accepted, but when the gentleman went to Blackpool to receive his number and permit, he was informed that steam cars would be debarred from competing, save in a class by themselves, and was offered the return of his entry fee. And he very rightly contends, the word "automobile "explains itself, and whether a vehicle is propelled by petrol, oil, electricity or steam, it is an automobile all the same. Therefore, if the races are for automobiles, one kind of car is as eligible for com- petition as another.

It is to be hoped that some sort of reasonable explan- ation will be forthcoming for the extraordinary conduct. Otherwise, if the matter be allowed to drop, one can hardly wonder that so many of the disappointed motorists, who had hoped to race their own steam cars, should declare that the trade element is far too rampant in the Automobile Club and that dangerous rivals are unscrupulously excluded from any competition wherein it is possible that they may shine.

The Duryea Company have also a grievance with refer- ence to the very same meeting. The ordinary rules were so amended that in the two-seated class only four- wheeled cars, Should be eligible. We believe that the Duryea is one of the very few cars entered for •speed trials which is not fitted with the regulation four wheels; but this is by the way. The price limit being advanced to £2so,the company removed the single steering wheel, and replaced it by a standard axle and pair of steering wheels, thus converting the phaatonette into a phteton. The car won its heat by 11 seconds,which was equal to about 400 yards in the mile—but a protest was lodged by the driver on the second car on the ground that the Duryea was too cheap for its class. The pro- test was upheld and the speedy Duryea was disqualified, in spite of the perfectly reasonable explanation offered and the fact that no four-wheeled Duryea phaeton, hav- ing, like this one, only the single circulation system, has yet been sold at a price exceeding £2so—the price limit of the class in which it was entered.

The fact of the matter is that, for some reason, very few of these race meetings appear to be managed in a satisfactory manner. If the rules which are supposed to govern them were to be clearly drawn up and then ragidly adhered to, no one would grumble but there seems to be too much changing at the last moment, partly owing to the careless phrasing of the regulations. and partly to the deplorable practice of some of the committee exercising a quality they refer to as their "discretion." There was a similar muddle and con- sequent dissatisfaction over the races in the Isle of Man last spring and the worst of it is that no one seems to learn from the past mistakes, for the mismanagement of the Phcenix Park races of last year should surely have taught a life-long lesson.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible, not later than Thursday. Sunday Trading. Sir,—As a Sunday school worker may I call the attention of all right thinking people to the great evil existing in our midst, namely—the opening of sweet and fruit shops on Sunday. There is one shop in particular in one of our principal streets, which is a crying dis- grace to our town. Young people of all ages throng the premises, and crowd the pavement all afternoon and evening. In school we teach the children the Command- ments of God. One of them is "Remember that thou keep holy the Sabbath day." How can we expect the children to remember our teaching, when they see so much frivolity and worldliness, and have as many oppor- tunities of buying sweets, &c., on Sundays as any other day. Let us loudly protest against Sunday trading in any form, remembering that " Six days shalt thou labour, but the seventh is the Sabbath of the Lord thy God." A SUNDAY SCHOOL TEACHER. Non-Traders and Alderman Smethurst,

—A Challenge. Sir.—l notice in the report issued by the Non-Trad- ing Committee that in their criticism of my father, Alderman Smethurst, the expression is used "until the transfer of the business to his son, 'which. is stated to have taken place four years ago." As this cannot fail to cast a doubt on a bona fide transaction I shall be pleased to subscribe the sum of £lOO to the funds of the War- rington Infirmary if the Committee can prove that what was done at that time was not a genuine sale—legally completed. If they fail, then they ought te correct their report and make such amends as are considered reasonable by all right thinking people, as insinuations of this kind can only be intended to cast doubt on the disinterested service of one who has done his level best for the good of the town for many years past.— Yours truly, JAMES HENRY SNIETEIRST. The Old Foundry, October 28th, 1904. Our Unforgotten Dead.

7 Sir,—The movement having for its object the erection of a memorial in honour of the officers and men of the South Lancashire Regiment (regulars and volunteers) who gave their lives for their country during the late war in South Africa has been well to the fore now almost 12 months and the labours of the Executive Committee under . the presidency of his Worship the Mayor and also a the Workingmen's Committee for the raising of funds necessary for carrying out the work will in a few days be finally closed. Response from many sources candidly has been dis- appointing. The committee with which it has been my pleasure to be associated have arranged a high-class concert in aid of this memorial in the Parr Hall on Saturday November sth, and as this object cannot but deeply appeal to every loyal and patriotic heart I am sure at this eleventh hour those who have not already given their support to the movement will do so by attending the concert referred to or sending in their donations forthwith. Yours faithfully, D. DAVIES. Chairman Workingmen's Committee. Town Hall, Warrington. October 27th, 1904.

===== 0002601_19041029_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
The Gondoliers.

Sir,—Who would have thought, a couple of years ago that the Warrington Musical 'Society comprised mem- bers of both sexes endowed with such exceptional opetatic ability as was evident last tear and again last week? The "Yeomen of the Guard" was first responsible for this pleasant surprise and now "The Gondoliers" has gone one better, if that is possible. For years we have been indebted to this society for good music, but good music isn't everything. Music less good, or less classical, is much more appreciated by the majority of concert-goers, and after all, what is most to be desired is the greatest pleasure of the greatest num- ber. That that result has been accomplished goes without -saying. Can anyone, who saw the thousands of happy perspiring faces in the Parr Hall nightly, have a doubt about it? In addition to the pleasure, there is the profit—monetary—to be reckoned with and no doubt that will be satisfactory too. They say that the chorus have lost lbs. during their practice for the caehucha so the society has probably gained Rs in'consequence, though "nobody seemed one penny the worse." A prettier, more melodious dance has never been seen in Warrington,l am sure. It would be unfair. on my part, to criticise the singing of the "principals." I am not unduly musical but I know when lain pleased, and they have pleased me as much asany stars "of the first magnitude ever did, and more than many. The fact that we are familiar with the appearance of the members in their ordinary attire,and count many of them as personal friends, made it specially interesting, but it required more than one visit to find "who's who." OPEREITE.

Mr. Keir Hardie's Reply to Rev. H. Dunnico.

81r,—The following is a copy of letter received from Mr. J. Kier Hardie, IV.P., which I shall be glad if you will kindly publish in your next Saturday's issue. Yours faithfully, A. WARBURTON. , Secretary Warrington Branch of the Independent Labour Party. COPY.

Dear Sir,—l have your letter enclosing cutting from the Warrington " Examiner " which contains a letter signed Herbert Dunnico. Evidently this gentleman is a diligent diciple of those twin pillars of Christian truth aud national righteousness Messrs. W. M. Thompson and Horatio Bottomly. In the postscript to his letter Mr. Dunnico says " Thefollowin manifesto with special preface by rg Mr. Keir Hardie M.P., was issued by the Indepen- dent Labour Party some. time ago." He follows what purports to be a quotation. Needless to say no such document was issued either as a manifesto by, in the name of, on behalf of or as in any way expressing the opinions of the Independent Labour Party. The statement that it was, however, is quite on a par with the rest of Mr. Dunnico's letter. If people will shut their eyes to facts and greedily swallow statements made by designing knayes intended to mislead honest fools, that is their own look out. I hope to have the pleasure of addressing a War- rington audience shortly under your auspices and will be charmed if Mr. Dunnico will then be able to make it convenient to honour the gathering by his presence. In these days of semi-enligh it is a positive pleasure to know that such tenment cal heathen as Mr. Dunnico appears to a politi- struts aboutlin all the glory of primitive ignorance.— Yours very truly,

J. KEIR HARDIE.

Local Authorities and Poor Children. Sir,—Will you kindly allow me through your news- paper to make a few remarks, suggested by a paragraph in the electoral address of the Independent Candidate for the vacancy in Bewsey Ward. Mr. Skelland states what is perhaps a unique claim for seeking a place in the Council Chamber; yet his argument is a humane and at the same time a logical one. I was impressed by the phrase, "I am [of opinion that fit is the duty of each community to study the welfare of its poor children, believing that care for the body and good influences on the mind of the young are powerful factors for good on the menand women of the future."

The truth of this assertion is apparent to every think- ing individual. The depravity and misery, which exist in all our manufacturing towns, cannot be satisfactorily remedied by dealing with the present adult generation of that class,with which these deplorable conditions are associated. Their condition is due in a large measure to their environment, and to other causes too complex for me to enter into in this brief letter. We must re mem- ber that the first Elementary Education Acts only came into force a little over 30 years ago,and for a long time the practical teaching was limited to reading, writing, and reckoning. Training the children to reason, and to grapple with the problems of life has only been attempted within recent years. If we admit the truth of Mr. Skelland's argument. the authorities of a community ought to act as powerful auxiliaries to the work of the elementary schools. E.g. It is cruel to compel a child to go to school on a cold winter's morning on an empty stomach, and then scold and perchance thrash it for being "stupid." Again, no child can be healthy whilst its body is allowed to go unwashed for indefinite periods. it is not onlyan easy prey to disease itself, but it is a grave menace to the cleaner child who is compelled to associate with it at the schools. Free baths for these children are then desirable, nay they are imperatively necessary, for the sake of the general health of all the children. If facilities were granted for free bathing, I feel con- vinced that the alarming outbreaks of scarlet fever, measles, etc., so prevalent in Warrington,would become far less frequent, and the present very high death-rate be materially lowered. The Ragged School authorities are to be highly com- mended for their efforts in this direction. They have constructed a large plunge bath, and a great number of poor children, boys one night, girls another, get a waeltly cleansing which otherwise they would not get. One department of the Corporation (through the schools) is doing excellent work in developing the physique of the children—the Gymnasium. The questions I have referred to concern the welfare of the body, but lif the neglected children of to-day are to emerge into a better class of men and women they must receive wholesome influences whilst their minds are, to use a hackneyed quotation, "like wax to receive and marble to retain." Here again our local authority can be a power for good through the medium

of another of its departments—the Library and Museum. This institution ought to be utilised more than it is, in supplementing the teaching of the Elementary and Technical schools. The Town Council might very well devote more of its time to considering the essential requirements of that section of the children of the town, who, out of school hours, are left almost entirely to their own resources. It would be time profitably spent, and a good investment. According to his address, Mr. Skelland is on the right track, and the burgesses of Bewsey will do a very wise thing if they return him as their representative. Apologising for trespassing so far on your valuable I space, I remain, yours faithfully, "DUTY."

===== 0002601_19041029_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
The Article in " Dawn."

Sir,—l ask you kindly to allow me to make use of your valuable space to make a few remarks with refer- ence to my sister's article which appeared in "Dawn." It is an easy matter to give a wrong impression of any article by simply publishing a fewportions without giving the context of the whole. This has been the case here, with the result that many have entirely mis- construed the purport of the article in question. I appeal to all respectable working men in Warrington to read, without preconceived prejgdice, the article as it appears in last week's "Observer 'and to ask themselves after having done so, the following questions: 1. Are not slums and slum-life a crying disgrace to our much-vaunted civilisation? 2. Is not drunkeness a great curse in this country? 3. Does not the physical, mental and moral develop- ment of the rising generation leave much to be desired? vfy sister has been blamed for taking Warrington as an example. It is natural however to talk about the town one knows best and presumably that is why she spoke about her own, without wishing to make it out worse than others,—we know of course that it is much in advance of many. We have not to go very far to find question No. 1 answered in the affirmative. I have often to go through the slums of Liverpool on my way to businass and I can safely say that the specimens of humanity one sees there are simply appalling. Now is itinot possible to avoid such wreck of mind and body as is here evidenced by wise national and municipal government? Of course it is ! Without discussing the subject of drunkenness, the evils of which need no delating on. let us take just one fact with regard to national physique: During the late war out of eleven thousand men offering themselves in Manchester for South Africa, ten thousand were re- jected as physically unfit to serve in the regular army. Shall we in view of this do well to smile self-com- placently and sing "no doubt but ye are the people." I ask you,sir,is it any good shutting one's eyes to these facts? Is it not better to have the courage (and I maintain it does require pluck in a young girl)to speak outand state things which are unpalatable in order to help to mend them by bringing them before the public? To read in what my sister has written a wholesale condemnation of the working class, is to read what is in no wise expressed in her article, and is simply to pervert the latter for party purposes. I am astonished that any working man should have imagined that such was Miss Cros- field's meaning. I believe that the family, to which I have the honour to belong, has done a little for the citizens of this town, and I assure them that no mem- ber of it has the welfare of the working people more at heart than my sister. It would be most unfair to attempt to make use either of this letter or that of my sisters for party purposes, and I take this opportunity of stating that as I am not at present in any way connected with either party whatever views I hold cannot be of interest to either Liberal or Conservative as sueh.—l am, sir, your obedient servant, • GEORGE R. CROSFIELD. Volunteer Quarters, Warrington. ' Oct. 27th, 1904.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield and the " Observer."

Sir.—At the same time that I wrote you last week I addressed a letter to your contemporary the "Observer." A single passage only out of this letter was published, and it by no means conveys to the readers of that paper what they would have gathered from the letter as a whole. lam afraid, to be quite frank, that the editor of that valuable paper has simply pickedout one brief sentence which suited his book,and ignored other passages which probably he did not relish, but which are quite as much to the point. Under the circumstances mas I ask you to publish the enclosed copy of the letter I sent and so help me to correct any wrong impression gathered by the readers of the " Observer " through the lack of courtesy—or shall I say courage—on the part of the Editor.—Yours A. H. CROSFIELD.

London, October 26th, 1904. [Copy of letter to the "Observer" Newspaper. October 21, 1904.] Sir' —I see that in last Saturday's "Observer" notes you have—doubtless for party purposes— dragged in my name in ab attempt to saddle me with responsibility for some passages which you quote from- an article in a certain Tory Journal. I read these passages with the deepest regret and I entirely disagree with them. Sad and weary faces there are while slack business swells the ranks of the unemployed, but we know how largely the reck- less and senseless foreign policy of the last nine or ten years, squandering the resources of the nation, piling up debt, disorganising finance, increasing taxation and seriously damaging the trade of the country—how largely this is responsible for it. You admire that kind of thing, but John Burns has rightly summed up the record of the last decade as a record of blundering and plundering. One can but deeply pity the unfortunate men, women and children who are the sufferers and take care we do not make matters worse by raising the cost—as you seek to raise it—for these poor people of the bare necessaries of life.—Yours &c., (Signed) A. H. CBOSPIELD. The Health of Warrington-0 up

Markets.

Sir,—Notwithstanding the publicity given to this matter by the publication of my letter on the subject, and your comments upon same two weeks ago, no action has yet been taken to effect the desired improvement. Were it not that the evil I complain of is a very serious one in many respects, I should have been con- tent simply to have drawn attention to the question, but, as I say, being a serious matter I think it my duty to take the matter up as best T may until some steps are taken to provide a proper remedy for the safe storing of the market rubbish in the way of fruit, etc. I notice that the same open tin bins are used and only the other night one of them was overturned and all sorts of rubbish lay strewn about in a small area. Any one going across the market might easily have walked across the heap and perhaps have slipped down over a part of it and sustained injuries. It was dark at the time. I might with justice make one or two remarks about the fish market and the way the place is cleaned out. Any person intimately acquainted with the locality round the fish market cannot fail to have been frequently attracted by the evil smells surrounding the place. Why these smells are so persistent Ido not know, for you can smell them at any time, even on a Sunday night after a day has passed since the trading on Satur- day night. Through what they arise lam not quite sure, although I suspect the place is not properly cleansed, notwithstanding the hose-pipe display which occurs in the early mornings. I do not know either whether the drains are in any way responsible—certainly there is a possibility. But there is at least one practice I along with one or two others have noticed, and it is this—that when the carts come round at night time to clear out the fish- refuse bins they are simply overturned into the carts and placed back into their original positions. The bins themselves are not cleaned at the time and the slime from the fish sticks to the sides of the bins making them very nasty and evil smelling. Now lam of opinion that when these bins are emptied they ought to be replaced by clean ones and the empty ones taken to a place to be properly cleansed before again replacing those which have taken their place. Perhaps other suggestions can be made whereby a better state of things could be brought about. We cannot shut our eyes to the fact that action I of some description is needed. I have also in conclusion a few words to say on the arrangement of the stalls in the fish market and in fact in both markets. For instance we notice in one place a cheese stall in very close proximity to a fish stall. Now this is anything but healthy and the wonder is that our ' keen-eyed medical authorities and nuisance inspectors have not noticed this. Besides, a classification Ischeme which allows all sorts and conditions of stalls to be jumbled up in all sorts of manners cannot be right. It is well known that there is some dissatisfaction among the stall holders themselves because of this slovenly method of setting out the stalls. May we ask whether the so-called meat market is utilised to the fullest extent? As far as I know,some of the stands are unoccupied and are likely to remain so. It must think be obvious that at least some reasonable scheme of classification of stalls should be projected, not only for the good of the stall-holders butalso inthe interest's of the ratepayers. I trust then that what I have written has not been written in vain and that something will be done. Again I ask where are our Labour men and Social Service leaguers? Where, oh where, are these aspiring people anxious for the welfare of the common classes?—Yours, L.W.E.

A Copenhagen telegram states that about half of the jewellery stolen from the premises of M. Michelson, Court jeweller, on Monday night, was found yesterday afternoon in a doorway. Most of the larger stones had been torn out of their settings. The property recovered is worth several thousand pounds.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 29, 1904.

THE INQUIRY REPORTS.

BIG RATEPAYERS' MEETING AT THE !PARR HALL.

Mr. S. Norhury Williams and the Findings of the Commissioners.

ALLEGATIONS OF THE NON-TRADERS .1 AaSOLUTELY PROVED."

Speeches by Mr. James Dodds, Mr. R. Cameron, M.P., anti Coun. Pemberton.

Evidence of the interest awakened by the publica- tion of the findings of the Commissioners who Conducted the inquiry into the trading transactions of the Corporation was afforded by the crowded attend- ance at the ratepayers' meeting held in the Parr Hall on Monday evening. This was the sixteenth of the series of meetings held in furtherance of the Non- Trading campaign, and the long reports which appeared in last Saturday's" Examiner" formed the chief topic for consideration. Mr. James Dodds presided and he was supported on the platform by Mr. S. Norbury Williams, Mr. Robert Cameron, M.P., Councillors Pemberton and Lord, Mr. S. Kenyon Mr. Longshaw and Mr. W. Benson. The attendance 'would number about 2,000.

MR. ALFRED HULME AND "DUST-THROWING." The CHAIRMA.N read a letter from Mr. Alfred anima regretting his inability to attend, owing to a family bereavement. He wrote: "I should like to have shown by my presence at the meeting that I still ho ld as strongly as ever the views so often expressed from the Parr Hall platform on this trading question, and I trust the ratepayers of Warrington, who are after all the original non-traders, will again prove clear-headed enough to see through the attempts of the traders and would-be traders to throw dust In their eyes."

===== 0002601_19041029_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
M. DODDS HAS NOTHING TO NIODIFY.

The CHAIRMAN said it was just three years since the effort for the purification of the administration of the municipal affairs of Warrington was launched. The pro- gress of the movement had been marvellous. The Inquiry which the ratepayers so emphatically called for was at last completed and they were there that night to welcome and to thank their three representatives who had been working so long and so arduously in their interests. (Hear, hoar.) Their report issued on Friday was a full and complete vindication of all that had been alleged at the many great meetings which had been held in that hall. Indeed the condition of affairs during the period immediately anterior to the commencement of the agitation was shown to have been in some respects even worse than they feared. He was not going to anticipate what Mr. Norbury Williams would say to them but for himself be desired to express his satisfaction that throughout the agitation he had said nothing that he had now to modify in any way whatever. (Applause). Everything he had said had been fully reported and every letter he had written had been over his own name (Hear, hear). Not a single anonymous word had eman- ated from him. He might say also, and truly, that neither publicly, nor privately had he said one word that could be twisted into a proof of personal enmity or animus or feeling against any man in Warrington. (Applause). He had never any such feeling and nobody knew that better,so far as he was personally con- cerned,than the gentleman who had lately from thewalls of the town been alleging the contrary.He( Hr. Dadds)had had to strongly criticise him and to criticise the public acts of some of their public men, and he stood by every word he had said. One splendid result of their work in and out of the Council was that there was now little if any improper trading. They must see to it that it was never again allowed to lift its head in Warrington. Mr. Dodds concluded by proposing that the best thanks of the meeting be accorded to Messrs. Horrocks, Pemberton and Norbury Williams for their services as Commissioners in the trading inquiry. (Applause).

Mr. S. KENYON seconded and the raltion was carried unanimously.

COUNCILLOR PEMBERTON AND THE INQUIRY Councillor PEMBERTON said he had been asked why the Non-traders in the Council had not done more to keep down expenditure. In this country he had never yet heard that the minority was responsible for what the majority did. The Non-traders as a party had never had control of the financial affairs of the town. All they had been able to do,with the support of the public,was to stop extravagant expenditure. The ratepayers could not expect their representatives to do their bidding in the Council unless they returned them in sufficient force to enable them to carry the majority. Another matter the ratepayers should take seriously to heart if they wanted to carry on reform was that they ought to support gentlemen who were willing to further that object with as little expense as possible to those gentle- men. Those who fought expenditure, cliquism and the people who went to Town Councils to get commissions and trade, had to pay their expenses out of their own pockets with no hope of return. On the other hand, men who got contracts and commissions could go to an immense amount of trouble and expense because they made sufficient to re- pay them. That was one reason why many men had taken no active part in municipal work. There were many things not actually included in the report which perhaps the ratepayers might have expected the Com- missioners would report upon. Although the conditions governing the inquiry were pretty wide they did not cover anything like the amount of trading that went on in the Corporation. The Council representa- tives called attention to the coal contract for the electricity works, but the Non-traders did not mention it because it did not strictly come within the terms of reference. Nor did many other matters. In the case of the coal contract alluded to, the Corporation bought £5,000 worth of fuel without advertising for it and at a much higher price,and he (Cortnoillor Pember- ton) could promise that at some future date more would be heard about the matter. Another serious matter to the ratepayers was that minutes of committees involving large expenditure were passed by the Town Council without non-members of the com- mittee knowing anything about them because they were not printed on the agenda. In one instance the sum of £5OO was to be expended, and probably members of the Council who voted for the minute were perfectly unaware of the item because they were not on the committee. That was a serious state of affairs and he believed it was really against the law. He would like to refer to the beautifulposter which their opponents had issued with reference to the trade of the town. It was a peculiar thing that the Non-traders should be making trade bad. Would the gentlemen who issued the poster suggest that they should build another police-station? (Laughter and applause.) Did they want another techni- cal school, another Hill Warren reservoir, or the sinking of more wells. Did they want a few small-pox hospitals? (Laughter). The poster only showed what " catch-penny " things could be placed on the walls. He had too much respect for the intelligence of War- rington people to suggest for a moment that such a bill could lead them astray. Mr. Norbuu Williams was deserving of their best thanks, for he had of late devoted more time to Warrington matters than even to Man- chester affairs. He had attended nearly all the sixty- nine sittings of the Commissioners and had put in about another, sixty attendances to prepare ''the report. (Applause.)

The CHAIRMAN said he could fully endorse what Councillor Pemberton had said with regard to the services Mr. Norbury Williams had rendered to the town. He did not think they would ever be able to repay Mr. Williams for the immense services he had rendered to purity in Warrington. He wanted Hr. Williams to realise that although they had not said very much in the way of thanking him they felt it deeply and they recognized that it was mainly by his aid that the tremendous improvement had been brought about in the administration of the affairs of the town. (Applause.)

SPEECH BY MR. NORBURY WILLIAMS. ALLEGATIONS "ABSOLUTELY PROVED."

Mr. NORBITRY Wrr.ura.ms,who had a cordial recent ion said that was their sixteenth meeting. He was always proud to recite the numbers of those meetings for the reason that he believed they might truthfully say that there had never been held in this country so grea a number of meetings so largely attended on any mu ni- clot question. For that reason he felt a pride in tak - ing part in that movement in the good old town of Warrington. Their report had now appeared before the public. He did not know what the public though of it and whether they had fully grasped the report or the meaning of all that was con- tained in it. He would like every burgess of Warring- ton to master all the details of the report and to try to understand what this direct and indirect trading in War rington had meant and, he thought he was right in say- ing, the harm it had done not only to the town but to the men who had taken part in the trading. (Applause). Mr. Dodds had alluded to the fact that Mr. Henry Roberts had upon the walls made a statemen t that the report had been purposely delayed. There was not a word of truth in that. These reports were not got out in the slap-dash way that some people got their reports out. (Laughter.) It was possible for some men to hear the evidence and finish the inquiry one day and to present the report the next day. The Non- Trading Commissioners had not found that possible, and at the same time to do their duty to the burgesses of Warrington. Therefore he wanted to tell Mr. Roberts and all others who made such allegations that there was

WHY TRADE WAS BAD

and they would have to go beyond the four corners of Warrington to ascertain that. They would have to look five or six years back and ask why the men of Warring- ton, Manchester and Liverpool and the whole country went mad five years ago on certain matters— (applause)—and landed them in a national:expenditure of 233 millions for which they had not practically a farthing of return. That was what they must ask. (A voice: Question). It was drawine,'a red herring across the scent to issue a ten foot placard asking why trade was bad. It was not because of the agitation—he could tell them that. (Applause). If they had had to pay a little that]the inquiry had cost them—and the amount up-to- date was £47o—they never spent better money. The man who spent a bit of money to clear his reputation when he looked back upon it had to admit that it was the best money he ever spent. When they had to meet that expenditure of less than £5OO, he said it was a good invest ment and they must not attribute to that the badness of trade in this town. There were a number of matters which were not mentioned in the report and to which he would like to refer. In one or two cases, for instance, they had been exceedingly displeased on find- ing that

PAPERS HAD BEEN' DESTROYED

or were missing. In several cases they had been unable to complete investigations in certain directions by reason of the fact that the documents which they should have to depend upon did not exist. That was one of the difficulties they had met with. Another point that had come out in the inquiry was that the minutes of the Corporation and its committees had not been all that the Non-trading Commissioners thought they should have been. They had found important. cases where no reference was made in the minutes, and 'they had found a number of other important cases to which the printed copy of the proceedings omitted to mention a large number of items and circumstances which they found in the written minutes. He wanted to impress upon them, as he had tried to impress i upon people n other towns, that the printed minutes of proceedings were not either in Warrington or in Manchester an absolutely correct and full report of the proceedings of the Council. In his judgment they did not come within the four corners of the law and the only document upon which burgesses should rely was the written minutes of the proceedings of the Council and its committees. The written minutes were the ones which were known to the law and it was the bounden duty of the officials of the Corporation to show them to the burgesses when they asked to see them. (Applause.) One did not like to adversely comment upon the acts and doings of one's colleagues with whom one had been sitting for a year, but he must call pointed attention to the fact that the six com- missioners who represented the Council in their zeal to serve dui Council and to help it, and it only, had for- gotten that the eleventh clause of the in- structions given to the commissions told them that "The Commissioners shall report their findings to the Town Council and to the Non-Trading Committee." In both cases, however, the Council' Commissioners had addressed their reports exclusively to the Mayor of Warrington. what was to him a clear index of what he had seen all through—the evident bias of their six colleagues, and their evident desire to do everything that could be done

TO SAVE THE FACE OF THE CORPORATION OF WARRINGTON.

That sub-contracting in the third or fourth degree reminded him of the house that Jack built. The sub-contractor was the malt which lay in the house that Jack built.lf they went on to the sub- contractor they found the rat which ate the malt which lay in the house that Jack built,and even if they went further back it all ended in the same thing in the house that Jack built. There was however a seriousness in those matters because one of their fellow-commissioners said there was nothing wrong in the sub-contracting. They might have set up a high standard of public action but they persisted in saying in their humble judgment that any person who was a member of their Municipal Council had no right to do business with that Council, and had no business to go one step further and supply the man who supplied the goods to the man who did work for the Council. (Hear, hear.) They did not care how far that gentleman might be removed. They main- tained that there must be no trading with the Council : either directly or indirectly. In Manchester if a mem- ber of the City Council was proved to have traded with the Corporation in the fourth degreelas a sub-con- tractor he was shown the door and told he was no longer wanted in the Council. They

WANTED TO RAISE WARRINGTON

to the same standard as Manchester, which he thought was as purely managed as any borough in the country. Another point in the report was that the Mayor's auditors, Alderman Bennett and Mr. A. J. Jolley had for years illegally received payment for auditing the Corporation accounts. The Council Commissioners said they found nothing wrong in this as the Town Clerk advised that the payment was legal. His (the speaker's) point was this, that the Local Government Act provided that no member of a Council should have any interest either directly or indirectly in the trading of that Council. Thatcvas a salutory provision without which trading such as they had set out to stop in War- rington would flourish throughout the country,and it was notin the spirit orthe Act that the Mayor's auditor should belpaid by the Council for his work. Mr. A. J. Jolley,for whom he had a great respect, admitted quite candidly that during the years he was Mayor's auditor he received £3O per year, and that a similar annual sum had been paid to Alderman Bennett during the time he had held that office. That, in the judgment of tke Non-Trading Commissioners,was wrongland they hoped it would not be paid again. (Applause.) A printed report had been issued with regard to that point in which 60 towns were named. In only eight of the towns, Warrington included, were the Mayor's auditors paid for their ser- vices. Speaking generally, all the charges they had ever made from that platform and all the statements they had made since the memorable day when Mr. Henry Roberts said the trading would go on, had been absolutely proved—(applause)_and that they had no reason to charge their memories or consciences with having put forth statements which were not true. Moreover they had found out things of which they did not know before. Their three colleagues had said that nothing had been proved but the public were the jury and they must read the two reporbz and say whether they thought the slate of Warrington had always been clear and clean or whether the Non-Traders were right. They said all their accusations had been proved and they would point out more, and that was that one of the chief facts brought out by the Inquiry was the disinclin- ation of persons who were connected with or knew about the trading to help them or to give evidence. He did not like to draw a picture of three men struggling to get at the truth (as he was. one of the men) when the whole town seemed to be against them and.when they asked for evidence not a single man came forward and offered to give evidence. He would go further. Thirty or forty men were written to including the chief traders—Alderman Francomb, Mr. Henry Roberti and Alderman Smethurst—and asked to come forward and give evidence and the whole string of them declined to come. THE NETWORK OF - COMPANIES

in this town had produced some results. When!he had set himself to make some little inquiry as to whether their three colleagues who were originally selected had any connections with any of these com- panies or any important men of the town—whether. in other words they were entirely free and clear and could enter dispassionately upon the inquiry—he had come across two or three rather awkward facts. They had bothered him a great deal. He searched the other day the list of shareholders in the "Warrington Guardian" and he found this curious fact which he could not 'account for: that Mr. Henry Roberts was found selling some of his "Warrington Guardian " shares first to Mr. Greening, one of the commissioners, and secondly to Mr. A. J. Jolley, another of the commissioners. (Laughter). He thought it was strange—plaintiffs, defendants and judges had got singularly mixed up. He did not quite know where they were, and he was glad he was on the jury. (Laughter). He went a little further and searched for the limited company of Messrs. Greeningand found .such familiar names as these :—Henry Roberts, J. A. Ewart, contractor, Alderman Smethurst and the Mayor of Warrington. He went further still and found that Commissioner A.J. Jolley's brother was connected with the Slate Company. These interlacings of interests in Warrington were marvellous, and when they had to examine facts of that kind and found that men who were sitting on the inquiry as commissioners and were supposed to be absolutely impartial had connections of the sort he had named, they begatelto wonder who there was in Warrington that was entirely free. Mr. Williams dealt with the sudden closing of tho inquiry by the original Council representatives and said the report they issued was like a damp squib. When their three friends had closed the shutters and gone home and issued their marvellous report which fell so terribly flat and whitewashed everybody, their other three friends came upon the scene and attendea thirteen sittings out of the sixty-nine. They had, however, issued their report on the whole sixty-nine. That was

'ONE OF THE FUNNY INCIDENTS

ef the campaign—that judges who had only heard. a quarter of the evidence pronounced their verdict on the whole lot. In their extraordinary report the Council Commissioners stated that it might be interesting to know that the total capital of the companies as shown by the return prepared from the registers at Somerset House was £11,709,004, whereas the value of the shares held by members of the Council and officials was only £134,001. If they were to go carefully through the companies of which the twelve millions of capital was composed, they would be sur- prised to see that several companies had several millions of capital and the shareholders who were interested had hardly more than a£s note in them. That was how these excellent gentlemen had made out those astonish- ing figures which ought to deceive no one, and he hoped they would not deceive the people of SN'arrington. The first of these companies was Armitage and Rigby, capital .E 2.90,000 total amount held by members of the Council—not a single penny. They could quite under- stand their three friends, anxious to pour contempt on the inquiry and to produce the effect they apparently had produced, bringing in such grotesque figures. A CLEAR ISSUE.

Nothing could be clearer, straighter or fairer,but Mr. Roberts retired and did not give them the chance to carry through the fight. They were hoping .that that day Mr. Henry Roberts would have been nominated, but when they came to examine the nomination papers he name of Mr. Henry Roberts was not there Mr. Dodds, the champion of their cause, had been nominated in seven wards, though Mr. Dodds did not know of that fact—(a voice: "Rats ")—in order to be prepared to fight Mr. Roberts, if he came out, in what- ever ward Mr. Roberts chose. - Mr. DODDS subsequently restored order and said they had no objection to people holdiir: opposite views be- ing present. He had been informed prior to the meet- ing that there would be present an organized repre- sentation of the other side. They were glad that they should be present but he would be greatly obliged if they would listen quietly and not interfere with the meeting. (Hear, hear.) Mr. WILLIAMS conti▪ nuing his speech said that though he could not deal fully with the report in the time allotted to him he felt sure that if the people of the town would only read that report they would be convinced that the volume and system of the trading which had prevailed in tip borough for years past, had not been good for the borough. The leaders of the Non-yrading agitation had not undertaken the work because they liked it but because they thought the trading which had been going on was wrong and therefore strove to put it right. No matter whether in municip• al or political life if there was anything going wrong we always found some man ready to give up his time to the righting of that wrong. That was why in England we had one of the purest forms of Government, both' Imperial and Local, in the world, and if any men deserved well of the country it was those men who had risen when occasion demanded and had given their time and trouble to set right the wrongs which existed. A great deal had been done in Warrington in that direction but there still existed
