Continuing with his commentary on the evidence Mr. Williams said that if there was a man to whom they were grateful for his evidence it was Mr. Daniel Cooper. (Applause.) He was a man who deserved well of Warrington. It required a great deal of bravery in Warrington to give evidence before the inquiry and

he was most grateful to Mr. Cooper for the manly part he had played in to some extent jeopardising his business by telling Ssomething which he knew of with regard to the purchase of bricks for the 'construe-1 tion of the palica buildings. Mr. Ceoper told a strange story which was not corroborated and which could not be corroborated because the man who had laid the case before them was one of the three who alone knew of what occurred. He said that one day he was asked to send a load of bricks as a sample to be used in the construction of the police buildings. He sent the sample and afterwards heard that they had been approved. A short tins afterwards he was called upon by two gentlemen from the Slate Company. He was at a funeral and the singular part of the case was that the two gentlemen, whose names were given, found him and went with him to the back parlour at the Rope and Anchor Inn, Waolston. There they began to speak about contracts which would be wanted for the police buildings and Mr. Cooper said they told him he could get the order provided he would allow a commission of is. per thousand bricks. (Shame). ,Mr. Cooper was not willing to give 19. per 1,000 corn nission and thought the matter over. Two days later he called upon Mr. Gordon Roberts and offered, to allow CI per 1,000 commission. tr. Gordon Roberts,in Mr. Cooper's words said "It was not worth putting through the books. If we could not allow is, which was little enough we could not get the order." (Shame). Mr. Cooper did not get the order and they found upon making further investigation that the order was given for Greengate bricks—bricks for the supply of which the Warrington Slate Company were the sole agents. He would ask the ratepayers to ponder over that case. Tile evidence given by Mr. Cooper was not supported because only three persons were present when the interview teok place and two of them were representatives of the Slate Company. Let them think over that matter. Let them think how it worked out and then say whether they considered it right or not. They were told that the cost of Greengate bricks was 3s. or 4s per 1.000 more than Mr. Cooper's bricks (Here there was another disturbance at the back of the Hall and cries of "chuck him out".) They desired to have the agency for the Greengate bricks stated at the inquiry and accordingly questioned Mr. T. Longdin, the borough surveyor, who pleaded ignorance—although he had been in Warring- ton 27 years—of what Councillor Wilkinson said was known by every little schoolboy in the town. Daring the questioning of Mr. Longdin, Councillor Wilkinson said Why waste time upon a point like that. Iths,s nothing whatever to do with the inquiry?"—A fair sam- ple of the sort of comment they had had made hundreds of times. The commissioners went through

THE EWART CASE

a little over two years ago Mr. Dodds, Councillor For- shaw and Mr. Ew trt. were in the train between War- rington and Liverpool when Ur. Evart made a very serious complaint to the twa other gentlemen. Cowl- cillor Dodds came before the Inquiry and stated what Mr. Ewart had said. Before Mr. Dodds went to the Inquiry he and another gentleman waited noon Coun- cillor Forshaw. Mr. Dodds read over to that gentleman his version of what had happened in the train. and Councillor Forshaw was asked if it was true. He replied than it was perfectly true, and moreover said he wag prepared to come forward and support that evidence before the Commission. He however never fulfilled that .promise, and even though four appointments were made, Councillor Forshaw never came forward to give evidence which would verify Mr. Dodds' statement. He thought Councillor Forshaw was seriously blameworthy when he said that the report was perfectly true, and afterwards failed to come forward to substantiate it. He did not think any words were strong enough to express his feeling upon Councillor Forshaw failing to come forward. He must, however, leave the ratepayers to deal with that gentleman. The statement was that on the 27th March, 1901, Mr. Henry Roberts' while Mayor of the Borough opened tenders for the tramways permanent way no other member of the Council being present at the time. They were unable to find any recorded minute authorising such opening. They regarded that as a very serious matter as there was absolutely no minute in the books of the Corporation to say that Alderman Roberts and Alderman Monks were authorised to open the tenders. In those tenders between 20,000 and 30,000 tons of materials were to be purchased, and they said that in their judgment it was contrary to public policy .that any man, whether he was Mayor of the borough or not, should open tenders by himself when he or his firm had any intention whatever of taking part either as a contractor or sub-contractor, in the work. They said that was wrong and was a thing which should not be allowed to happen. (Applause.) They asked Mr. S. Wilson whether that sort of thing often happened and he replied that he did not think that on more than two or three occasions had it been .done during the last 50 years, and as a matter of fact he (the speaker) believed they could not find another single instance of such a thing having been done. The thing was quite excep- tional and on that ground they objected to it. One singular part was that whereas the tender was opened by Mr. Roberts on the 29th March, they found that the next day the Warrington Slate Company wrote a letter to Messrs. Walter Scott Limited, Leeds, who tendered as sub-contractors to Mr. Ewart and several other tendering contractors asking for their best merchant terms for steel rails to specification to the extent of about 750 tons. What they objected to was that the same gentle- man who opened tenders should be the gentleman who was writing to the sub-contractors or that his company should be writing for prices to this Leeds firm. They said that was entirely wrong and in their opinion was contrary to public policy. Two days later they found that after the opening of the tenders by his father, Mr. Gordon Roberts waited upon Mr. Ewart, as he himself had shown by appointment. According to his letter to the commissioners dated 23rd June this year, the Slate Company furnished, some weeks before, quotations for various materials to Mr. Ewart and a number of other intending contractors and the interview of March 30th, 1901, hag reference to those quotations. According to the statement made by Mr. Ewart to Messrs. Dodds and Forshaw the object of the visit of Mr. Gordon Roberts to Mr. Ewart,(who did not then know the tenders bad been opened, nor that his tender was the lowest) was to induce Mr. Ewart to substitute the materials of the Slate Comnany forfthose ,of the firms whose names and goods Mr. Ewart had !included in his tender as required by the ierms of the Corporation specifications under which he had tendered. It appeared to them that unless Mr. Gordon Roberts knew that Mr. Ewart had not included the name of the Slate Company nor their goods in his tender, he could not possibly have any other object in waiting, upon Mr. Ewart than that stated by the latter. From their report the public would see that there had been things proceeding in Warrington which should not have been. The Non-Traders had endeavoured to point those things out and it now remained for them to see that the men who sought to enter the Council were such as coald be relied upon in the matter of trading. (Applause.)

MR. DODDS PREPARED TO MET MR. ROBERTS.

In introducing Mr. Cameron to the meeting, Mr. DODDS said he wished to put the audience right regard- ing a point in the report of their opponents, which stated that the cases of Mr. W. Roberts and himself (Mr. Dodds) were exactly alike, both being paid secretaries of companies trading with the Corporation. But there was this difference : That whereas be had no interest whatever in Pearson and Knowles', Mr. Roberts was a considerable shareholder in Heskeths. To have been in the same position as Mr. Roberts, he (the speaker) ought to have had £14,000 in Pearson and Knowles. ;If Mr. Roberts had been only the secretary of the company his name would not have been men- tioned. Applause.) He might inform his friends at the back of the hall, representing the opposition, that he was opposed to the policy of his being nominated in those seven wards—and they knew it—so that to cry "rats" as they had done early in the meeting was trying to throw discredit upon him and was not worthy of men. (Applause.) It was not worthy of the men of Warrington who knew that all his services to the town had been given at his own ex- pense_ On the County Committee on which he served he was for thirteen days going up and down the vari- ous districts and revising the assessments. He had paid h;s expenses—amounting to some- thing like £2o—out of his own pocket and sent the allowance made to him for expenses to the Infirmary—(applause)—because he believed that a Councillor ought never to receive even his own expenses. If he woe going to give his public services to his constituents, he should "give" them—(applause) —and it would never be in their power to say that he (Councillor Dodds) had ever received alpenny for what he had done for the town asilong as he lived.(Applause.) He was prepared to fight Mr.Roberts.not because he was Mr. Roberts, but because he wanted to have a fair issue put before the people of V4arrington once and for all. Did they want purity of administration or not? If they did they would vote for him, and if not they would vote for the other man. (Applause.) If he did not get ten votes he would be content, and would go home with the peace in his heart that passeth all understanding, because he would have the consciousness that he had stood for a principle which was above all victories municipal or otherwise. (Applause). SPEECH BY MR. CAMERON, M.P.

Mr. CA ERON M.P., said that a few weeks back he had a small report sent to him in London. That was the first side of the question, and there that night he had heard the other side and the reply to the first. In the report sent to him there WASI an item underlined in red ink, so as to pall his special attention to it. The moment he siw that item he confessed he was a little alarmed to find that of those trading with the Corpora- tion Messrs. Pearson and Knowles traded to the largest extent. Now he knew his friend Mr. Dodds was con-

nected with that firm and he thought there seemed something queer about the thing. He enclosed the re- port and posted it off to Mr. Dodds immediately. He got a long letterin reply and he was sure the satisfac- tion he felt when he read it was the • satisfaction any- one would naturally feel who knew Mr. Dodds. (Applause). It told him that he (Mr. Dodds) was not a shareholder in that or in any other company trading. with the Corporation whilst a member of the Council, and that all the trade which Pearson and Knowles did with the Corporation was legitimate and perhaps natural-and inevitable. (Applause) A Corporation like theirs must, of course, trade with somebody, but if a member of the firm they traded with was in the Council and directed that tenders should go to his firm, then there was corruption. That was the whole essence of the business. (Applause.) As regraded Corporations generally it did require vigilance on the part of somebody if something had been done that was based upon selfishness and a desire to take advantage of one's position. If that had been the case it was a serious sign of the moral condition of the members of the Corporations. He would say to voters that if there had been corruption to any extent the fault was primarily with those who pat the men in the Council and did not watch them afterwards. He knew nothing of the personality of the Warrington controversy, but he knew that it was very important for them if they were to have proper • manicipal government in this country that anyone who was elected to a public position ought to have clean hands and a public motive for entering upon the duties. There would never have arisen all this stir and these charges if there had not been something going on. There was never smoke unless these was a fire or something behind it. (Applause.) He had knowal Mc. Dodds for many years, and did not hesitate to say that corning from a stalwart race of ,orthumbrians, he was not one who, for the sake of personality or ambition or for the sake of any disturb- ance would undertake such a disagreeable task as this. (Hear, hear.) He had undertaken it in the public interest and for the good of W trrington, and he had had to bear something for doing it. Ile had had to go through and suffer many vile imputations; low motives had been imputed to him—and the labour he and others had had to share during their efforts to bottom thi3 business ought to commend the character of Mr. Dodds. It had been hinted that he (Mr. Cameron) might move a vote of thanks to him, and he did so with the full conviction that they would have to go far before they would find a man. who would face all the opposition and bitterness he (Mr. Dodds) had had to go through on their behalf. (Applause). He did not know whether Mr. Dodds wished to go into the Council again or not, but this he did know, that it was such man as he that they ought to have in the Council. (Cheers). The public opinion of the country must denounce self- interest whether it was found in Parliament or in Cor- porations. Democracy had its dangers. It was made up of men,who were tomptible, and the ratepayers ought to watch them and see that the temptation was not too strong for them. They must demand from their repre- sentatives moral qualities that would not allow them to stoop to make gain from. their positions. (Cheers.) Councillor LORD seconded the motion which was en- thusiastically carried. in returning thanks. Mr. DODDS' said his services were ,at their disposal, but he wld not sat' in the Council. 'He thought he could do as t s', t.)c them outside the Council as inside it. Bat wh ',over his decision might be at present, he would fight the battle when necessary against a leader of the other side.

MIDLAND IRON TRADE. .

[FROM OUR SPECIAL CORRESPONDENT.]

BIRMINGHAM, THURSDAY. There was a decidedly poor attendance on 'Change at Birmingham on Thursday, and the state of trade cer- tainly does not justify the glowing reports of improve- ments and an-flowing orders circulated in certain quarters. Orders are a little heavier, and works are more nearly approximating to full time, but with one exception there is no material improvement in prices, about which there is much complaining. The single exception is galvanised sheets which are ss. dearer owing to the fact that within the week spelter has advanced 15s. a ton. Pig-iron is about sixpence dearer than a month ago, for certain brands, but many makers fail tolget this increase. Stocks of pigs are genemllysmall, a circumstance which will stimulate a rapid rise if a demand sets in. Garman and American steel bars for sheet-hiaking are beaten out of the market by South Wales, this district monopolising this branch of trade. The Welsh article is Is. dearer, but users, having regard to the quicker delivery, do not mind paying this. Local steel houses for finished lines are busier, and orders for rolling stock are coming in rather better. The genera view is that if foreign complications do not arise, a steady trade may be looked forward to until the end of the year:

===== 0002601_19041029_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
Midland Wages Board Crisis. The Protest of Outside Firms.

The serious crisis in the position of the Midland Wages Board was the subject of some comment on Thursday on 'Change at Birmingham. A good deal of anxiety as to the Board's future was expressed. Outside firms repudiate with some warmth the statement that they are paying lower wages than those under the Board, and this will form the subject of sharp controversy in the immediate future. Association workmen equally deny the reflection on Yorkshire, Lancashire, and especi- ally Warrington, as to the receipt of lower wages, and assert that the trouble is most serious in the Midland district. A Northern correspondent writing on the subject the other day said referring to these countries." To say that they are working under Mid- land wages is a lie and a libel." Some strong comment on the matter is being made at a series of meetings be- ing held by the men throughout the Midlands. The. last of these takes place to-day (Saturday) at West Bromwich, and these are to be followed by -neetings in Shropshire. Sir Benjamin Hingley, the chairman of theßoard, in- formed our Board representative that a number of replies had been received by the secretary Mr. Daniel Jones to the circular addressed to Midland firms invit- ing them to join the Board. These had not yet been analysed, but a special meeting of the Committee would be held early in November and a report submitted to the Standing Committee at the end of the month.

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON.

ENDING OCTOBER 22nd, 1904. During the week, the following births and deaths were registered :—Warrington Registration District. births, 39; deaths, 18. The excess of births over deaths was 11. The birth-rate was equal to 29.7. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 13.7 per 1,000 against 21.3 the average of the previous 4 weeks and against 154 the corresponding period of last year. The death rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 2-2 per 1,000, against 77 the average rate of the. previous 4 lweeks and against 6•9 the corresponding period of 1903. The ages of mortality were :- 0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-63 65—up. Totar 7 1 1 1 6 2 18 The deaths included-1 from scarlet fever, 2 front diarrhoea, 1 from phthisis, 11 from other tubercular, I from cancer, 2 from lung iseases, 1 from accidents, 9 from all other causes. The following cases of infectious disease have been reported during tile week :—Scarlet fever, 34 cases Diptheria, 1 case; erysipelas, 3 cases. J. GUEST GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS. 1904. TO THE ELECTORS OF LATCHFORD WARD. L &DIES AND GENTLEMEN.— At the request of several ladies and gentlemen. I have decided to offer myself as a Candidate for your Ward at the coming Municipal Elections. Should you do me the honour of returning me I shall do my utmost to prevent, all useless expenditure ; at the same time any really necessary improvement I shall be prepared to favourably consider. I think no one should benefit from membership of a publics body; also that there should be impartial treat- ment of all tenders for public work. I am in sympathy with the Fair Wage Clause being strictly observe• in all Corporation Contracts. I am a large ratepayer and reside in the Ward. I am, Ladies and Gentlemen, Yours obediently. WILLIAM BENSON. 96, Wliderspool rd.. Latchford. and (Draper) 11. Sanhey St. October 26, 1904.

M"Iel PHALLIC C T (11"01FI 0 w s RY w. ;TROD THE LADIES AND GENTLEMEN, I have been requested by many Ratepayers and Bur- gesses to allow my.elf to be nominated as your repre- sentative in the lo .vn C aincil for Bewsey Ward. After mature consideration I have consented, and now respectfully ask for your support and votes on the day of election. I have resided in this Ward for 45 years. and am well known amongst you. and have an Intimate knowledge of the conditions and requirements of the district. I am the nominee of no political or organized parties, but come before you as an Independent Candidate, actuated so!ely by a desire for the w Hare of the town and its inhabitants, more especially those of the poorer class. I have noticed with concern the prolonged bad trade and the resultant distress which becomes more acute each winter. I would therefore support any wise and practical measures which might be broos;tit forward to aid the unemployed. I am of opinion that It is the duty of each community to study the welfare of its poor children, believing that care for the body and good influences on tha mind of the young are powerful factors for good on the men and women of the future. As you are aware. I have all my life taken a deep Interest in the poor children of Warrington, and during the last 18 years practically the whole of my time has been devoted to their service. This has boen done with- out receiving any monetary remuneration whatever ; it has been a labour of love. No one knows better than myself the continuous round of privation and misery there children have to endure. Thousands of children have been fed and clothed and received good Christian Influence through their connec- tion with the Ragged School, of which lam the Super- intendent, and ninny have been rescued from despair and crime and are to-day respectable law-abiding citizens. I would, therefore, take every opportunity of pressing forward this vital question, and strongly support any scheme that would tend to better their lot. RM in favour of :- 1. Free Breakfasts for School Children during periods of local distress, so that every poor child can have a meal before going to school (scores of children in Warrington often went to school last winter without breakfast). 2. Free Baths for Children. Our experience with the bath at the Ragged School (although payment for the water used is demanded by the Water Committee) shows that this privilege would be an inestimable boon to all the children whose bodies, for want of home convenience, are sadly neglected in this respect. Free Baths for children are also a Sanitary Necessity in Warrington, when we consider the great proportion of disease and death amongst them,anet which materially help to swell up the mortality returns. NOTE.—Warrington has lately had the unenviable distinction of having the highest death-rate in the country. 3 lam a strong supporter of the Fair Wage Clause I being inserted In every Corporation Contract. 4. Anything that would teed to Better Housing of the Working Classes would have my active support. 5. lam in favour of Halfpenny Stages on the Trams and of a Reduction in the Price of Gas. I would oppose all extravagant and unnecessary expenditure such as has been going on for some years, and which has brought our rates up rapidly to the pre- sent alarming figure to the detriment of local industries and to the employment of our workmen. In conclusion, I beg to say that I have no personal ends to serve. lam solely impelled by a desire to help my fellow-townsmen to the best of my ability, and by a regard for the best interests of the town. I respectfully ask your votes and sapport on the day (A the election, and, if returned as your representative, will try to prove myself worthy of your confidence. I am, Ladies and Gentlemen. Your Obedient Servant, JAMBS SKRLLAND. Bewsey street, October 25th, 1904.

THE NORTHERN UNION. NORTHERN LEAGUE. First Division, _ WARRINGTON v. WIGAN. The ever-exciting match between Warrington and Wigan at Wilderspool was played on Saturday before a fine crowd of 8,000 people. Beautiful weather favoured the event and a fast, keen game followed, -though play was not always of a high standard. Both teams were I representatiye, Harris turning out for the home team f for the first time since the opening match with Leigh! when he sustained an injured knee. The teams were : Warrington—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son, and Harris, three-quarter backs; Hockenhall and Davies, half backs; Thomas, Cook, Belton, Shugars, Morrison, Preston, Jolley. and Naxio r, for wards. Wigan—Mason, back; .Leytham; Harris, W. Jones and Lowe, three-quarter backs; Anderson and Tresize, half backs; Collier, Hilton, Barton' Vickers, Fryer,lOsborne, Whittaker, and Blears, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. J. Dance, Morecambe. Touch judges, Messrs. H. Davies, Flixton, and J. Graham, St. Helens. Hilton started for Wigan, and the opening phases of the match went in favour of the visitors. Eventually Hockenhall broke away, and Dickenson rushed over only to be recalled for some informality. The attack was maintained, and Anderson being penalised, Preston landed a fine goal from a difficult position. Wigan then made a prolonged visit to the opposite end, but their efforts lacked finish, and Warrington again took up a strong position, Hockenhall failing with a clear field before him. By easy stages Wigan got out of a tight corner, and after Windsor Jones had failed to drop al goal, Leytham placed a penalty goal, and at half-time) the score was two points each. Turning round, Warrington at once took up an attack- ing position and though Leytham drove the locals out, Fish was instrumental in placing Warrington again on the aggressive with a long run and , after Davies had failed near the line, Preston landed a penalty goal. The home forwards now forced the pace, and near the close Preston forced his way over, and also added the goal. The ex-Swintonian thus scored all the nine points accruing to Warrington, and received an ovation on leaving the ground. Final :—Warrington, 3 goals 1 try (9 points) ; Wigan, 1 goal (2 points). • WIDNES v. ST. HELENS. At Widnes, before 4,000 spectators. St. Helens won the toss, and Curtis started for Widnes. Alf. Taylor mulled badly, but was able to recover himself, and put in a beautiful run to within a few yards of the line. The Widnes backs got going, and Wailing was almost over the line when he was pushed into touch. St.Helens gradually worked their way into the home half, and when Tilley wasabout to save, he lost possession, and Prescott had nothing to do but ground the ball. Tray- nor failed from an .easy position. Half-time score : St: Helens, 1 try (3 points) ; VVidnes, nil. Immediately on restarting Tilley was collared on his own line, and from the subsequent scrummageEdinonds got:possession, and passed out to Hall, who dropped a goal. After this St. Helens had the best of the game, and Widnes were kept on the defence. As the result of a good passing movement, Traynor scored at the corner but Jones failed to improve. -Just before the finish' Widnes pressed, but could not score. Result :—St. Helens, goal 2 tries (8 points); Widnes,nil. Teams:— Widnes.—Tilley, back; Wailing, J. Taylor, A Taylor, and Barber, three-quarter backs; Kennedy, and Jolley, half bacl;s ; Argent. Curtis, Cockram, Lynch, McCabe, McDonald, Turner, and Webster' forwards. St. Helens.—Jones, back: Hunter, Traynor, Devereaux, and Barton, three-quarter backs: Edmonds and Hall, half backs; Briers, Prescott, Thompson, Whiteley, Sherwood, Mooney. Wharton, and Maverty, forwards. Referee, Mr. Gibson, Salford. Touch judges, Messrs. T. J. Elliott, Birkenhead, and J. Simm, Wigan. IiVAKEFIELD TRINITY v. OLDHAM. At Wakefield, before 5000 spectators. The play was mostly confined to the forwards, and in this depart- ment Trinity had the advantage. Spottiswood scored a
